BMW Just Went 186 MPH In The New Alpina 7 Series

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Thanks to spy shots, we already knew BMW was hard at work cooking up the next Alpina B7 and that it'll have a V8 that sounds pretty great. The brand has now come out with official footage of the car doing high-speed testing at the Nardò test track, exceeding 186 mph. What, did you think the special label version of BMW's flagship sedan would be slow? For reference, the last-gen Alpina B7 already had a top speed of 205 mph, so 186 was frankly table stakes. That said, the speed itself arguably isn't what BMW is flexing her — it's the peace with which that speed is achieved.

"Speed has always been baked into the Alpina brand's DNA," said BMW Alpina vice president Oliver Viellechner. "However, we are not just interested in top speed alone. What matters is how natural, stable and confident it feels to drive at that speed." Internally, the brand apparently calls these cars "distance-shrinking" machines; autobahn weapons made to cover literal continents in great speed, style, and serenity.

You might recognize the name Nardò, by the way, from "Nardo Gray," that primer-like paint color popularized by Audi. The Nardò Ring, a 7.8-mile test track in southern Italy owned by Porsche and shaped like a perfect circle, is where that comes from.

Other confirmed details now include Alpina-signature 20-spoke lightweight wheels (peeping at the sidewall shows a diameter of 22 inches) wearing specially made tires that feature an extra layer of foam inside allowing for peak high-speed stability and comfort. Four oval exhaust tips emit a "rich, deep sound," and BMW says max torque out of that V8 is available "across virtually the entire rev range," while engine management software and throttle mapping is tweaked for smoothness over aggression. Gearbox tuning, too, has been revised to accommodate the bump in horsepower and engine speeds.

This new B7 (or whatever BMW will choose to call the next Alpina-ized 7 Series) will be the first production car made under the joint BMW Alpina brand. BMW announced that it had acquired Alpina back in 2022, but didn't take full control over the tuner until the beginning of this year. The new BMW Alpina 7 Series will be unveiled next year.

ChatGPT can now make animated GIFs with OpenAI’s Images 2.6

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Users can turn a prompt or photo into a GIF, then change its speed and edit single frames.

The ChatGPT app open on an iPhone, its dark start screen showing the OpenAI logo.

OpenAI is adding animated GIFs to ChatGPT with a new image model, Images 2.6. Users can make a GIF from text or a photo and edit its frames. Developers get two new API models, and OpenAI says people now make over 4 billion images a week.

OpenAI is rolling out animated GIFs in ChatGPT alongside a new image model, Images 2.6, The Verge reported on Wednesday. Users can turn a written prompt or an uploaded photo into a GIF.

Once a GIF is made, users can change how fast it plays, look at and edit single frames, and share it. Until now, ChatGPT could not make a GIF in one step. The feature arrives months after OpenAI shut down Sora, its AI video app, in March.

OpenAI says Images 2.6 also makes image generation in ChatGPT faster. It is rolling out to all ChatGPT and Codex users on the web, desktop and mobile.

Developers get two new API models: GPT-Image-2.6 Flare for speed and GPT-Image-2.6 Sunburst for quality. They follow the split OpenAI introduced with Images 2.5 in September, when Adobe put one of its models into Firefly.

Use keeps growing. OpenAI says people now make more than 4 billion images a week across ChatGPT and its API, up from 3 billion in September. The figures are OpenAI’s own and cover all image use, not only GIFs.

OpenAI has updated its image tools often this year. Images 2.0, launched in April, was its first image model that reasons before drawing. Last month, OpenAI began testing ads inside image generation in the US.

The Real Ark of the Covenant May Be Hiding in Ethiopia—and Only One Man Alive Is Allowed to See It

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

According to an ancient Ethiopian epic, the biblical relic was stolen in a 3,000-year-old temple heist—and now sits in a tiny chapel guarded by a single monk for life.

Next to the Church of Our Lady Mary of Zion in the ancient city of Axum, Ethiopia, stands a small, fenced-off sanctuary called the Chapel of the Tablet. Entry is forbidden to everyone on Earth except one man: a lone monk appointed for life to watch over its contents.

According to believers, he’s guarding one of history’s most sacred and sought-after relics: the Ark of the Covenant.

In the Hebrew Bible, the Ark is described in precise detail: a rectangular chest of acacia wood overlaid inside and out with gold, measuring roughly four feet long by two feet wide and high, and crowned with two golden cherubim with outstretched wings. Forged at Mount Sinai to house the Ten Commandments and serve as God’s literal throne on Earth, the Ark guided the Israelites through the wilderness, toppled enemy armies, and eventually became the sacred centerpiece of King Solomon’s Temple in Jerusalem. Then, during the Babylonian destruction of Jerusalem in 586 B.C.E., this ultimate seat of divine power vanished from the biblical record without a trace.

For millennia, its disappearance has fueled endless archaeological quests, conspiracy theories, and pop-culture touchstones like 1981’s Raiders of the Lost Ark. Yet according to the Kebra Nagast (“The Glory of the Kings”), a revered 14th-century Ethiopian national epic, the Ark was never lost at all. Instead, it was smuggled out of Jerusalem nearly 3,000 years ago by Menelik I, the son of King Solomon and Makeda—better known outside Ethiopia as the Queen of Sheba.

According to the epic, Menelik grew up in Ethiopia before traveling to Jerusalem in his early twenties to meet his father, carrying a royal ring Solomon had left with his mother as proof of his lineage. Solomon recognized the young man immediately—he was the spitting image of his grandfather, King David—and begged his favored son to stay and inherit the throne of Israel.

Menelik insisted on returning home to rule his mother’s kingdom. Reluctantly, Solomon agreed, but on one condition. The firstborn sons of Jerusalem’s highest-ranking nobles, priests, and military commanders were ordered to accompany Menelik as his new royal court to establish a “second Israel” in Ethiopia.

That decree sealed the Ark’s fate. Devastated at the thought of leaving the presence of God behind, the young elites conspired to pull off an ancient inside job. The heist was masterminded by Azariah, son of the High Priest Zadok. Prompted by an angel in a vision, Azariah commissioned an exact wooden replica of the Ark. Under cover of night, he and his co-conspirators slipped into the Temple, swapped the genuine Ark for the decoy, and hid the real relic in their luggage.

When Menelik’s caravan departed the next day, the Kebra Nagast claims the Ark’s power lifted their wagons and animals off the ground, propelling them toward Ethiopia at supernatural speed. By the time Zadok discovered the counterfeit and Solomon’s army gave frantic chase, the Ethiopians were long gone.

Christianity was still centuries away, so as tradition has it, the relic was first kept on Tana Qirqos, a remote island on Ethiopia’s Lake Tana, where priests guarded it for 800 years. Then around 340 C.E., when King Ezana of the Aksumite Empire converted to Christianity, the Ark was ceremoniously moved to the newly built Church of Our Lady Mary of Zion in Axum, permanently linking the ancient Israelite covenant with Ethiopian Christianity.

Over the centuries, whenever invaders threatened Axum—including Queen Gudit’s forces in the 10th century and the devastating Adal War in the 16th century—monks spirited the Ark away to mountain strongholds and island refuges until the cathedral could be rebuilt. In the 1960s, Empress Menen Asfaw, wife of Emperor Haile Selassie, funded the dedicated Chapel of the Tablet next door. According to local monastic lore, the move was necessary because a divine “heat” radiating from the Ark was cracking the ancient stone walls of its previous sanctum.

Because the Ethiopian Orthodox Church forbids anyone—including scholars, archaeologists, and even the church’s own patriarch—from laying eyes on the relic, its authenticity remains impossible to verify scientifically. Only one outsider in modern history has claimed to get a look. In 1941, British army officer Edward Ullendorff—later a prominent professor of Ethiopian studies—allegedly used his military clout and fluency in classical Ge’ez to talk his way inside the sanctuary. In a 1992 interview, Ullendorff claimed the chest he saw was merely a “middle- to late-medieval construction.”

However, Ethiopian officials and historians quickly pointed out a flaw in Ullendorff’s scoop: every church in Ethiopia keeps a replica of the Ark, known as a tabot, for ceremonial use. If Ullendorff saw a medieval wooden box, believers argue, he was simply fooled by a decoy—the exact same trick Azariah used on King Solomon 3,000 years ago.

And so, while treasure hunters continue to scour subterranean tunnels in Jerusalem and desert caves across the Middle East, millions of Ethiopians believe the search is already over. The world’s most famous lost treasure isn’t buried in a secret vault—it’s sitting right on a street corner in Axum, hiding in plain sight.

Shawn Gorman is Deputy Editor of News for Popular Mechanics. Before joining Pop Mech, he specialized in academic reading for educational testing. His diverse academic background also includes time spent as a lecturer and librarian at institutions such as the University of Kentucky, SUNY, and Harvard University. Shawn holds a Ph.D. in Modern Foreign Languages and Literatures from Boston University, an M.A. from The Ohio State University, and a B.A. in History from the University of Oregon. His favorite subjects to explore include computer science, physics, math, and biology. He lives on a medium-sized cat ranch in Easton, Pennsylvania, with his wife and two daughters.

Data Center Backers Suffer Near-Total Midterm Defeats Across the Country

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

More than 40 voting initiatives yielded almost nothing but resistance to data-center projects.

The 2026 midterm election blue wave, which shifted the balance of power in Congress and across state races toward Democrats, can be seen as a repudiation of AI and the controversial data centers that provide its computing power. Data center proponents have heavily backed President Donald Trump and Republican candidates, underscored by xAI CEO Elon Musk investing hundreds of millions of dollars into Texas midterm campaigns to support the industry.

But in many parts of the country, voters didn’t need to make their voices on data centers heard through candidates. They had direct initiatives on the ballot this week that would decide whether data center projects would move ahead in their districts.

In line with a growing backlash against data centers and AI, voters supported about 40 election-day measures that would restrict the construction of new data centers, with only one result — in Harwood, North Dakota — favoring data center expansion.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, CNET’s parent company, in 2025 filed a lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

As broken down by the website Data Centers on the Ballot, Ohio had the most number of voting measures: 21, all of which were in favor of restrictions.

Maine, Illinois, Kansas, New Jersey, California, Florida, Nevada, North Dakota and Wisconsin were the other states with data center voting.

Six elections were held earlier in 2026 in California, Michigan, New Hampshire and Wisconsin, with all of them finding voters opposed to allowing data center builds to continue without new restrictions.

Not all the proposed restrictions were the same. In some elections, voters decided whether to impose a megawatt cap on data center energy use, while other measures addressed specific zoning issues or whether data center projects should require voter approval. Some proposed an outright ban on new data centers, and six of those were successful.

Polls by groups including Pew Research have found Americans have soured on data centers due to high electricity bills, water use and environmental concerns, among other reasons. It’s also hard to separate concerns about data centers from concerns about AI affecting jobs. AI has also become a divisive enough issue to affect midterm voting.

The election results were so bad for the industry that Politico declared data centers to be “the midterms’ other big loser” next to Republicans. Not only did voters enact additional restrictions on builds across the country, but data centers were mentioned in Democratic victory speeches.

“I do not work for data centers. I do not work for Wall Street. I work for you, I work for Ohio,” Senator-elect Sherrod Brown said in his victory speech Tuesday night.

USA Today reported that two mayors, one in Oklahoma and one in Ohio, were recalled after public backlash over proposed data centers.

The midterms might have longer-term effects for tech companies and data center firms. It’s likely that with Democratic control of Congress, there’ll be more scrutiny.

“Public policy is going to reflect public opinion, and there’s been a very substantial change in public opinion just over the last year,” Darrell West, a senior fellow at the Brookings Institution, told Politico.

Political headwinds from the midterms could lead local officials across the country to gain public favor by delaying or canceling data center projects or putting pressure on the companies behind them to offer more attractive deals to communities. The days of huge tax breaks and incentives for data centers could be coming to a close.

“Now, (communities) are pushing data centers to pay taxes, create their own power plants, pay for their use of electricity and water and contribute to local community resources like education funds,” The New York Times reported.

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time's Navi actor reprises her role after 28 years, but it was a total accident due to Nintendo's blind auditions

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

For The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time's shiny new remake, Nintendo unintentionally cast the same actor twice - 28 years apart - to deliver Navi's infamous "Heys!" and "Listens!"

The Switch 2's Ocarina of Time makes a pretty substantial change to the N64's Ocarina of Time, released almost three decades prior. There's proper voice acting this time, of course - not just grunts and murmurs and hyahs. But even when retroactively adding new things, Nintendo wanted to ensure the remake's cast matched the original's pretty faithfully, so the developer held blind auditions that eventually paved the way for one actor to reprise their role during Link's adventure.

"We felt that if we were going to improve the graphics so dramatically, the cutscenes needed to be fully voiced to match," director HokWai Ng explained in the most recent Ask The Developer blog. "But at the same time, we knew we shouldn't mess with established perceptions of how certain characters sound based on the original. Navi's "Listen!" is one example. Even someone who's never played The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time may have heard it before. We absolutely didn't want to change anything as iconic as that."

To maintain the original's nostalgic sounds and voices, Nintendo held blind auditions with the goal of finding actors who could recreate the "feel of the original game."

Series director Eiji Aonuma recalls that the team wanted to choose actors "based solely on which voices most closely resembled those of the original." And, who woulda guessed? "As fate would have it, one of the actors we selected had actually voiced the same character in the Nintendo 64 version. None of us saw that coming."

The duo didn't outright name which character they're talking about, but fans have long been speculating that Kaori Mizuhashi might be reprising her role as Link's trusty fairy companion Navi - at least, in the Japanese language version. A leaked online video of the game's credits now also confirms that Mizuhashi is back almost three decades later. I suppose it only makes sense that the person who most closely resembles Navi is Navi.

Chris Donlan called the new version an "astonishing remake" in his five-star Eurogamer Ocarina of Time review.

Alisson FUMES at Liverpool team-mate Jeremy Jacquet after Reds star turned his back and deflected Mason Greenwood's free-kick beyond goalkeeper

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Published: 14:15 EDT, 4 November 2026 | Updated: 18:19 EDT, 4 November 2026

Alisson is usually composure personified for Liverpool but lost his rag with Jeremy Jacquet on Wednesday night.

The Brazilian gave his team-mate both barrels after Fenerbahce's equaliser - a heavily deflected Mason Greenwood free-kick.

Jacquet was at the edge of the Liverpool wall and twisted his body to take evasive action, with the ball ricocheting off his midriff and past the goalkeeper.

Jacquet has been a shining light for the Reds in his debut campaign but it was a poor moment that left his stopper livid.

Replay footage showed Greenwood's powerful attempt appeared to be heading wide before the deflection off Jacquet completely changed the trajectory.

Alisson had been moving swiftly to his left before being wrong-footed and the ball flew off the wet surface to his other side.

Alisson was raging at Jeremy Jacquet's poor blocking attempt for the Fenerbahce equaliser

Jacquet's expression indicated he knew he'd made a poor mistake for the goal

The 34-year-old's fury was immediate and he screamed at Jacquet, gesticulating wildly in his direction.

The centre-back appeared to recognise his mistake and brought his hand up to cover his eyes on a grim evening in Istanbul.

On commentary for TNT Sports, Ally McCoist said: 'It's Jacquet and he's livid (Alisson), does he turn his back? He certainly does, you've got to face it.

'The goalkeeper is livid, absolutely livid and with justification as well. He's (Jacquet) is side on for someone who has had a good start to the season.'

Jacquet has been partnering Virgil van Dijk in almost every game so far this term for Andoni Iraola's men and acquitted himself excellently.

Having joined from Rennes in the summer for £55million after agreeing the move in January, he's seamlessly replaced Ibrahima Konate, who left on a free transfer before signing for Real Madrid.

Jacquet is still just 21 and the France international will no doubt want to avoid the ire of his goalkeeper and learn his lesson when it comes to forming a solid wall.

Trey Nyoni had opened the scoring for the Reds before Greenwood's leveller. Alexander Isak then came off the bench to give the visitors the lead once more before Fenerbahce roared back.

Two goals in the space of six minutes gave the Turkish side a 3-2 lead in the pouring rain, to the delight of interim boss and cult Liverpool legend Dirk Kuyt and the home side held on for a famous win.

5 Common Mistakes That Are Making Your Vintage Decor Look All Wrong—and How Designers Get It Right

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Experts say the best rooms mix eras, styles, and stories.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pictured above: This space, designed by Sarah West, strikes the right balance of vintage and contemporary decor, proving that a mix is always better than sticking to one design era.

It’s not just the Instagram algorithm playing tricks on you: Everyone really is looking to antique and vintage decor. Homeowners are growing tired of rooms that look as though everything was bought from a single brand in one fell swoop. We’re now at a point where a collected, curated, personalized feel has replaced quiet luxury.

“I think it’s important to add at least some kind of antique or vintage piece in every room,” says Sarah West, founder of Sarah West & Associates and a designer for over 20 years. “Even if you’re doing a more modern or transitional room, a sense of history adds weight to a space.”

It’s not necessarily as easy as plopping one antique into a room and calling it a day, however, and that can feel intimidating. Even once you learn to shop for antiques and vintage like a pro, what do you actually do with those finds when you’re home?

While the designers we spoke with are quick to point out there really are no strict rules here—it’s about your style and what you love—there are a few key mistakes that pop up most often when people start styling with antique and vintage decor. These designers have the scoop on how to avoid those missteps and make your treasures, and your entire room, shine.

Committing to a massive piece of furniture or tasking yourself with overhauling an entire room with antique or vintage pieces can get overwhelming fast. You may make decisions you don’t love down the line.

If you decide you’re going to decorate a whole room in a limited amount of time, and you’re set on sourcing antique or vintage items, you’ll likely end up settling for things just because you need them, says Nancy Cavaliere, a New York–based interior decorator, stylist, and vintage collector. Go slow and start small.

“I’d rather live with an empty corner while I wait for the perfect piece than fill it with something I don’t love,” she notes. “The most interesting homes evolve over time. I think a room should tell the story of the person living in it, not the story of one shopping trip.”

Ease your way into decorating with vintage and antiques by starting small. Think one piece of art, one mirror, one vase.

“Don’t overthink it,” West advises. “Take a small piece, try it out in the room for a couple of days, see how it feels.” Do you like the piece in that spot? Does it work with other elements of the room? Does it inspire other styling choices for the space? Try moving it around and interacting with it to find its perfect positioning and see where that takes you.

As you start out getting a feel for that smaller piece, remember that just because it’s unique doesn’t mean it has to be the focal point of the whole room.

“Don’t treat vintage pieces like they have to be the star of the room simply because they’re old,” says Cavaliere. “Not every antique needs a pedestal. Sometimes the most beautiful thing you can do is let an extraordinary piece sit comfortably alongside something completely ordinary.”

Fully incorporating vintage or antique pieces into a space imbues the room with that richness and intrigue.

“These pieces give feelings to a room,” West says. “If you put something in a corner, as you transition throughout the room, it gives this element of surprise.”

This includes creating high-low mixes, says Cavaliere, who points out, “a $20 flea market find can look incredible next to an expensive designer piece. It’s all about how you put things together.”

In fact, that mix is something to strive for, says Alex Bass, founder of Salon 21, Art Advisory & Interior Design Firm.

“I love a contrast, like a room with modern architecture that leans heavily into antiques or vice versa. If you want to incorporate pieces from Pottery Barn, CB2, that’s fine, but there are also so many affordable vintage finds. And something like an antique light fixture can make all of these pieces work.”

Speaking of contrasts, all of our designer sources say a very common misconception they encounter is that you can only decorate a room with pieces from the same era.

“Just because you have a Victorian chair doesn’t mean you need a Victorian living room,” Cavaliere says. “My own home is a mix of different periods, styles, colors, and patterns, and that's what gives it personality. I love the tension between pieces that seemingly have nothing in common. And it's fun!”

Bass cites the example of Art Deco being popular right now, while articles are claiming midcentury modern is “out.” She encourages people to think of pieces less rigidly, assuming they have to commit fully to one style, or toss another style because someone declared a new one the next big thing.

“These are not ‘trends,’ these are design periods that already existed,” she explains. “It’s about having the eye to pull pieces from different periods that speak to you and make sense in your room.” You can mix Art Deco and midcentury modern, for instance, by using elements characteristic of them both, like marble, warm woods, mixed metals, and bold color pops.

There can be a fear with vintage and antique pieces that changing anything “ruins” them. But unless it’s a significantly valuable piece you’re investing in or something you plan on having appraised, Bass says, embrace the ability to make special pieces work for your space.

“Don’t be afraid to reupholster,” she says—and not just if the fabric is in poor shape. “If I buy a piece and the structure is beautiful but I want to add a contemporary fabric and print, that can make for a fun contrast with the frame.”

For example, Bass fell in love with a modular midcentury sectional, but its fabric didn’t quite complement the space she was designing, so she chose a contemporary fabric that worked with its aesthetic.

This, Cavaliere points out, frees you up from obsessing over condition when shopping for pieces. If something has great bones but its fabric or finish has seen better days—and if you love it—don’t pass it up. Remember you can reupholster, strip and stain or paint, and repair.

Just factor the cost of those fixes into the price of the item to help make your decision. Especially because, as Bass advises, it’s better to consult a professional unless you’re well-versed in certain projects. Stripping and staining a wood piece is fairly easy, but when it comes to reupholstering and other repairs, you’ll likely actually save money by outsourcing to a pro rather than potentially damaging the piece yourself.

Similarly, it’s typical for people to feel they can’t actually make use of vintage and antique pieces, because they think they’re too precious. But especially considering how well-made many older pieces are, they’re begging to be put to work. Again, it’s actually incorporating them into your everyday life that gives your room and entire home that sense of personalization, curation, and storytelling.

“One hundred years ago, people were using their silver,” West says. “In my kitchen, you’re eating with hotel silver.”

This includes getting creative with how you use antique and vintage pieces—don’t feel bound by what something is “supposed to” be for.

“One of my favorite tricks is turning vintage drapes and valances into bed canopies,” Cavaliere says. “I did this in my daughter’s room and I absolutely love it.” Cavaliere also finds unexpected uses for small vessels and decorative objects: a creamer and sugar set in the bathroom to hold Q-tips and cotton balls; dishes, trays, and crystal bowls to sort accessories in her closet.

“A vintage china cabinet doesn't have to live in a dining room just because it was designed to hold dishes,” she says. “One of my favorite tricks is using china cabinets to display and store shoes and handbags in a dressing room or closet. Why spend a ton of money on custom cabinetry when you can repurpose a beautiful vintage piece that has so much more character?”

West agrees. “Purchase an armoire, flip the doors, add mirrors, and make it a bar,” West suggests. “Find an old door knocker you love? Hang a towel off of it. Use a tablecloth to make a pillow. Tell a story.”

OpenAI Pipeline Finds 948 Linux Kernel Privilege Escalation Flaws

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

OpenAI reported on November 4, 2026 that a prototype pipeline using an upcoming cyber model found and exploited 948 unique local privilege escalation vulnerabilities in Linux during a mid-October 2026 research run, while also generating candidate patches for maintainer review.

The flaws could give an unprivileged local user root access. Testing took place in isolated virtual machines running a stock desktop operating system with SMEP, SMAP, KASLR and HARDENED_USERCOPY enabled, rather than a deliberately weakened target. OpenAI chose Linux because the kernel contains more than 40 million lines of code and underpins Android devices, cloud infrastructure and supercomputers, providing a test of automated research against a codebase of that scale and importance.

The pipeline connects vulnerability discovery, dynamic validation, exploitability assessment, patch generation and human review. Scout agents select code based on its reachability by low-privileged users and security relevance, after which Analyst agents scan the targets. Triage agents generate and refine proofs of concept inside virtual machines running kernels with Kernel Address Sanitizer, preserving runtime evidence rather than relying solely on static analysis. Each completed research bundle contains a root-cause analysis, a minimal crashing proof of concept, a candidate patch and a full exploit.

OpenAI said the Scouts selected 5,034 audit targets covering about 2.8 million lines, roughly 7% of the kernel. Analysts produced 2,308 deduplicated candidate findings within 12 hours, and dynamic validation reproduced 1,724 vulnerabilities within 30 hours. Most remaining findings required elevated privileges or could not be reproduced within the time budget; only 3.1% reflected incorrect model analysis.

The exploit stage produced 291 information-leak proofs of concept and 1,169 working local privilege escalation exploits, according to OpenAI. Variant analysis then produced 948 distinct patches, and OpenAI uses that figure as the number of unique LPEs, explaining why the vulnerability total differs from the raw exploit count. The generated fixes remain candidates for maintainer review; the 948 figure is not a count of merged patches.

OpenAI has sent hundreds of reports and patches for critical vulnerabilities and plans to distribute the remainder gradually with Patch the Planet partners. OpenAI introduced the Daybreak initiative with Trail of Bits on June 22, 2026, pairing AI-assisted research with expert review while maintainers retain control over patch deployment and disclosure. The earlier announcement reported 24 LPE exploits and eight kernel-pointer information-leak proofs of concept from its GPT-5.5 Cyber Linux work.

After finding 61 LPEs already patched upstream, 43 with staging patches and 299 being publicly reported or discussed at the end of the ten-day scan and manual analysis, OpenAI recommended revalidating findings against current code, including later merges, to avoid duplicate or obsolete reports. The goal is to focus maintainers on unresolved vulnerabilities without adding unnecessary review burden.

Review also caught a failure that a root shell alone would have hidden. In an early run, OpenAI said 20 of 200 reported successful exploits obtained root through a different 0-day than the assigned vulnerability, after which it tightened task constraints and added an independent check of each exploit’s path. Early directly prompted patches could be functionally correct while containing unnecessary code, potential performance tradeoffs, departures from coding conventions or incomplete variant coverage. Additional instructions and iteration improved patch quality, while human researchers still review the exploit, patch and proposed severity before reporting to maintainers.

OpenAI’s comparison puts the mid-October run at $1.213 million in API credits, or $1,279 per unique LPE, against 166 unique LPEs and $513,690 for a late-July 2026 GPT-5.6 Sol run. Both scanned the latest Linux kernel master branch available at the time. The reported more-than-fivefold increase in novel LPEs came despite three months of hardening between scans. The costs are presented as upper bounds because the research focused on methodology and capability measurement rather than cost optimization.

OpenAI said a sample of 166 exploits contained ten techniques that both its team and third-party kernel exploitation experts believed were new, spanning persistent elastic objects, page grooming, data-only privilege escalation and arbitrary type confusion. Full technical details were withheld for safety reasons.

An adapted kernelCTF run produced 25 information-leak proofs of concept and 21 LPE exploits over 112 hours at roughly $32,936 in API credits, OpenAI reported. The tested kernel was incompatible with the SLAB_VIRTUAL mitigation, so OpenAI removed it from the configuration. The roughly $1.5 million bounty figure is conditional on all 21 vulnerabilities being accepted, rather than an announced award. Google’s kernelCTF rules list a $71,337 reward per eligible exploit, require at least 70% stability across 20 runs and limit rewards to qualifying 0-day submissions.

The results move the bottleneck from generating leads to proving exploit paths, keeping findings current and producing acceptable fixes. That makes independent verification and maintainer review central to whether automated research at this scale becomes usable defensive work rather than an additional reporting burden.

@Balaam_ That's a pretty elementary conflation of two entirely different meanings of discrimination. @dskatter Transparency is a different issue and agree it's important. Otherwise, price differentiation is a good thing for consumers. For example, an anime game might sell for $70 in Japan because demand is particularly strong there, but $50 in the U.S. because consumers are less willing to pay the higher price. The publisher can capture more revenue in Japan while making the game more affordable to American consumers. The benefit is that one universal price might leave money on the table in Japan while pricing out potential customers in America. Differential pricing allows the company to serve both markets rather than treating their different demand conditions as identical. P.S. Do you find it immoral when your grandparent gets a movie discount because of their age even though you are seeing the same movie? Or a veteran discount?

My Ski Helmet Looks Perfectly Fine. Here’s Why I’m Replacing It Anyway.

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Last February, on the final day of SKI’s annual ski test, I caught an edge in variable snow conditions while testing carving skis. I went, as we say, over the handlebars, landing directly on top of my head and compressing my neck.

I knew I’d hit my head, but at the time, I didn’t think much of it. It wasn’t until the following day that I began to understand how hard I’d actually hit the snow.

A few days later, I was sitting in my doctor’s office being diagnosed with a concussion and a neck injury. For months afterward, I dealt with headaches, neck pain, and sensitivity to light and sound. Some nights I’d wake up at 3 or 4 a.m. with pain radiating from the base of my skull, unable to get comfortable enough to fall back asleep. The hardest part was how unpredictable it all felt. I’d think I was getting better, only to have the symptoms flare up again.

The good news? I was wearing a helmet that may have spared me a much more serious head injury. The bad news? Now I have to throw that helmet away.

If I hadn’t developed concussion symptoms, I probably wouldn’t have thought twice about wearing it again. My helmet was brand new, and from the outside, there’s no obvious damage. But apparently, that says little about what’s going on underneath.

Needing a little extra convincing to toss out what looks like a perfectly good, well-fitting helmet, I turned to Madelen Fahlstedt, Ph.D., a biomechanical specialist at MIPS, a helmet-safety technology company. Fahlstedt holds a doctorate in medical engineering from Sweden’s KTH Royal Institute of Technology, where she researched head-injury biomechanics and accident reconstruction.

She explains that most ski helmets contain a layer of expanded polystyrene (EPS) foam designed to absorb impact energy by compressing or deforming. Once it takes a serious hit, that deformation is permanent, meaning the affected foam can’t absorb the same energy again.

“The outer shell can hide these deformations in the EPS. Even from the inside of the helmet, the deformations can be hard to detect,” Fahlstedt says. “To that sense, a helmet is essentially a one-hit-wonder.”

Traditional ski helmets are designed primarily to absorb the energy of an impact, particularly the forces associated with linear motion, when your head strikes something and comes to an abrupt stop. These forces can cause serious injuries, including skull fractures.

But real-world ski crashes aren’t always so straightforward.

When your head hits the snow at an angle, it can also rotate suddenly. That rotational motion can cause different parts of the brain to move relative to one another, stretching delicate tissues.

“The brain is typically more sensitive to rotational motion than linear motion because it has shear properties similar to water or gel,” Fahlstedt explains.

Both linear and rotational forces can contribute to brain injuries, which is why some helmet manufacturers have introduced technologies designed to address rotational motion.

MIPS, for example, doesn’t manufacture helmets. Rather, it develops safety technology that manufacturers can incorporate into their designs. So you might find a MIPS system inside a Smith helmet or a Giro helmet, even though the two models look, fit, and function differently.

Its best-known technology allows a layer inside the helmet to move about 10 to 15 millimeters during certain angled impacts. That small amount of movement is designed to reduce the rotational motion transferred to your head. Depending on the helmet, the layer might be visible or built into the padding.

MIPS is just one approach, however, and no technology can guarantee protection against concussion.

In fact, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention is clear that no helmet is concussion-proof. Still, helmets provide meaningful protection. According to a 2022 review by the American Academy of Pediatrics, skiers and snowboarders who wear helmets have roughly 34 percent lower odds of suffering a head injury than those who don’t.

According to Fahlstedt, a helmet should be replaced after a significant impact, regardless of whether there’s visible damage.

The challenge, of course, is figuring out what counts as significant.

A helmet that rolls off a tailgate or gets knocked against the chairlift safety bar isn’t necessarily in the same category as one that absorbs the force of your head hitting hardpack. But a crash doesn’t have to look or feel dramatic to involve substantial forces.

Mine certainly didn’t. Without the concussion diagnosis, I’m not sure I would have considered it a significant impact at all. But as the CDC notes, concussion symptoms can take hours or even days to appear, so how you feel immediately after a crash isn’t necessarily a reliable indicator of injury. If you suspect a concussion, stop skiing and seek medical evaluation.

The same cautious approach applies to your helmet. When in doubt, err on the side of caution. Based on Fahlstedt’s advice and helmet manufacturer guidance, here’s when to replace your ski helmet and what to look for when buying a new one:

These standards establish minimum safety requirements, but they don’t tell you how one helmet compares with another. For that, Fahlstedt points to Virginia Tech’s Snow Sport Helmet Ratings, one of the most respected independent resources for comparing helmet safety.

Its ratings include 56 snowsports helmets and evaluate their performance in laboratory tests measuring both linear and rotational head motion. Rather than simply passing or failing a safety test, helmets receive a one- to five-star rating, giving skiers another way to compare their options. You can compare those ratings with SKI’s 2027 Winter Gear Guide, where we selected the eight best ski helmets for resort and backcountry skiing from 36 models we tested. Our hands-on testing evaluates factors like fit, comfort, ventilation, and usability on the mountain.

It’s tempting to look at nearly new gear, especially something that wasn’t cheap, and convince yourself it’s probably fine. But a helmet isn’t like a scratched pair of goggles or a jacket with a torn sleeve that can make it through another season with a little duct tape.

Mine has already done its job once. I’m not asking it to do it again.

Swift had a pretty good 22-year run, but NASA wanted the scope to keep going even longer.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

NASA's Swift space telescope has reached the end of its long and winding road.

Swift fell uncontrolled to Earth today (Nov. 4), 22 years after it launched but just three months after a private spacecraft was supposed to boost it to a higher, life-extending orbit.

The 3,200-pound (1,450 kilograms) observatory reentered Earth's atmosphere at 7:39 a.m. EST (1239 GMT) today over northern Sumatra, according to satellite tracker Jonathan McDowell, who cited U.S. Space Force data. The fall likely didn't make much of an impact; NASA officials predicted beforehand that most of the minibus-sized Swift would burn up as it fell, and they put the odds of any of the surviving pieces hurting anyone on Earth at just 1 in 2,100.

Swift — officially known as the Swift Gamma-Ray Burst Explorer — launched to Earth orbit in November 2004 on a two-year mission to study gamma-ray bursts (GRBs), the most powerful explosions in the universe. "Swift" was not an acronym but rather referred to the spacecraft's observational agility.

"Swift needed to detect and locate gamma-ray bursts, communicate their position to other observatories, and then swing its telescopes around to image the burst locations before their afterglows faded out," S. Bradley Cenko, Swift's principal investigator (PI) at NASA's Goddard Space Flight Center in Maryland, said in an Oct. 23 statement. "This typically took Swift about a minute to do."

Swift didn't just observe GRBs. It also studied a variety of other cosmic bodies and phenomena, from faraway supernova explosions and feeding black holes to asteroids and comets in our own solar system.

And it kept doing this long after its warranty expired — for about 20 years after, in fact. The space telescope didn't conduct its final science observation until this past Oct. 20. (Swift underwent a name change during this stretch: In 2018, it was rechristened the Neil Gehrels Swift Observatory after its first PI, who died the previous year.)

"Swift delivered an incredible, lasting return on investment for the nation, designed for a two-year primary mission but productive for more than 10 times as long," Shawn Domagal-Goldman, director of NASA's Astrophysics Division, said in the same Oct. 23 statement.

"In that time, Swift played a major role in advancing our understanding of the changing universe and was part of a revolution in astrophysics that showed how critical rapid-response observations are to the entire community," he added.

Swift was incredibly productive, observing more than 2,000 GRBs and 1,400 supernovas over the course of its long scientific life. But it could have gathered even more data, had a dramatic rescue mission worked out.

In September 2025, NASA awarded the Arizona company Katalyst Space a $30 million contract to design, launch and operate a robotic spacecraft that would meet up with Swift and boost it to a higher orbit, thus extending its operational life.

Swift was falling back to Earth: It had no onboard thrusters to counteract the effect of atmospheric drag, which had been pulling the observatory down ever since it launched. The process was accelerating as Swift got lower, so Katalyst had to work fast. And the company did: It launched its refrigerator-sized, three-armed LINK servicing spacecraft on July 3, targeting an August capture of Swift.

That didn't happen, however. LINK suffered problems with its propulsion system and orientation-maintaining reaction wheels, which nixed the planned meetup. Still, it was a worthwhile effort, according to NASA.

"From the beginning, this was a high-risk, high-reward mission," Domagal-Goldman said in a statement on Sept. 25, the day that LINK itself crashed back to Earth.

"And while we'll be sad to see Swift's mission come to a close, we knew this boost effort would be worthwhile either way — advancing U.S. spacecraft servicing technology, challenging us to meet unprecedented mission timelines, and testing how we operate satellites to extend their time in low Earth orbit," he added.

Swift's crash today wasn't exactly unusual, by the way. At least three large objects — dead satellites or spent rocket bodies — fall back to Earth every day on average, a 2025 report found. And that number is bound to rise in the coming years, as humanity builds out more and more megaconstellations in Earth orbit.

He primarily covers human and robotic spaceflight, military space, and exoplanets, but has been known to dabble in the space art beat. His book about the search for alien life, "Out There," was published on Nov. 13, 2018. Before becoming a science writer, Michael worked as a herpetologist and wildlife biologist. He has a Ph.D. in evolutionary biology from the University of Sydney, Australia, a bachelor's degree from the University of Arizona, and a graduate certificate in science writing from the University of California, Santa Cruz. Airbnb’s Brian Chesky Says He Was ‘Naturally a Terrible CEO.’ Here’s What Makes the Job So Hard — And What’s a ‘Death Sentence.’

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

The Airbnb chief executive opened up about his shortcomings as a leader.

Brian Chesky has been the CEO of Airbnb since the company’s inception in 2007. For nearly 20 years, he has stuck with the top job and overseen Airbnb’s incredible growth to become a company with over 5.5 million hosts and $12.24 billion in annual revenue in 2025.

However, being a CEO didn’t come naturally. In a recent interview with the How I Built This podcast, Chesky opened up about his shortcomings as a leader when Airbnb first got started.

Chesky drew a distinction between being a great founder and being a great CEO.

“I think I was pretty much naturally a great founder and I was naturally a terrible CEO,” he said on the podcast.

Chesky pointed out that someone could spend their “whole life” being a founder. It starts in childhood, with making and building things — all prep work for being a founder. The moment a person creates a company is probably not the first time they try to make something, Chesky said. He estimated that when he started Airbnb at age 26, he probably had “20 years of experience as a founder, starting as a child designing, disassembling Christmas toys.”

By the time he founded the company, he claimed to have had decades of experience with founding or creating something under his belt.

Then came the tougher challenge: being CEO. Chesky said on the podcast that the main problem with being a CEO is that it is so counterintuitive.

“For example, people tell you to hire great people and trust them and get out of their way to do their job,” Chesky said. “That is what you do once you are very mature. That is a death sentence to a young company.”

Chesky said that good CEOs actually have to be strategic in making new hires. They have to steer away from hiring experienced people who don’t understand the company. Instead, they have to hire workers a “little bit ahead” of them who are smart and hungry — and then “stay really close to them.”

Chesky also addressed the question of how CEOs can create a culture where employees automatically do what is expected of them, even when the CEO isn’t in the room.

“That’s the hard stuff,” he said. “That’s really hard. Not becoming profitable, expense management — that stuff is easy.”

Chesky admitted that early on, he was “cautious” about being a boss. He “felt shy” about directing people to do something, especially workers who seemed older and more experienced.

“I think I was a little bit of a people pleaser,” he said. “I wanted people to like me.”

The difficult part for Chesky was when his intuition would clash with what his employees were telling him. Usually he deferred to their experience, and usually it was a mistake, he said.

“My intuition is telling me this thing,” Chesky said. “It doesn’t mean you’re wrong, but now let’s talk about it.

What Is It Supposed to Feel Like to Push Your Limits? 5-Time CrossFit Games Champ Mat Fraser Knows.

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

No pain, no gain? The GOAT shares his perspective.

MAT FRASER UNDERSTANDS what it’s like to push your limits. But the five-time CrossFit Games champ found the motivation to train to failure at the 2015 CrossFit Games, when he finished in second place. Fraser remembers feeling devastated—finishing as a runner-up felt like a personal offense to his purpose and identity.

“Once I made that commitment to get back in the gym and give it another shot, I kept that moment fresh,” Fraser says in the latest episode of Strong Talk. “Knowing that you don't get that beautiful moment in front of thousands of people without the thousands of moments by yourself in your gym when no one's patting you on the back and no one's cheering for you.”

What did it feel like to push his limits in his workouts? Fraser says he’s on the right track when he’s “tasting pennies,” the top of his head is tingling, and his heart rate is over 200 bpm. “Leave no doubt on the table, and be proud of myself. Then no matter what results I get, I know that's what I earned,” he explains.

But despite his preparation, Fraser is only human. Despite his impressive accolades, he never got rid of the pre-event jitters. Fraser tells MH fitness director Ebenezer Samuel, C.S.C.S. and exercise physiologist and strength coach Dr. Pat Davidson that he used to dry heave or grab a bucket as his breakfast came back up before every competition. In the episode, the trio delved more into the hardest workouts he did to prepare for the Games, what it's like to train recreationally versus for competition, and how to exercise and fuel your body when you only have 15 minutes.

Van Gogh’s Starry Night shows a real night sky, study suggests

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

For decades, art historians and astronomers have wondered whether the sky of Vincent van Gogh’s The Starry Night shows real stars or imagined ones. A new study argues the stars are real — and that Van Gogh likely saw them a month later than most scholars thought.

The study, published Oct. 22 in Scientific Reports, comes from a team led by Elijah Jeong-hyun Kim, CEO of the South Korean telescope maker SLLAB and a Ph.D. candidate at Kyung Hee University’s School of Space Research in Yongin.

By combining astronomical data, historical primary sources, and statistical analysis, the team narrowed the celestial alignments shown in the painting’s sky to a handful of late-July mornings.

They further suggest Van Gogh likely painted the eastern sky as seen from his asylum room in Saint-Rémy-de-Provence, France, around 3 a.m. on July 22, 1889. That morning, a waning crescent Moon, Venus, and the Hyades — the V-shaped star cluster in Taurus the Bull — were all gathered in the same small patch of sky.

RELATED: Exploring the solar system through space art

In May 1889, Van Gogh admitted himself to the asylum of Saint-Paul-de-Mausole, a former monastery outside Saint-Rémy. The previous December, after a falling-out with fellow painter Paul Gauguin, he had cut off his left ear, and more breakdowns followed. At the asylum he kept working, producing some 150 paintings, including famous works like Almond Blossom and The Starry Night.

On June 18, he wrote to his brother Theo that he had begun “a new study of a starry sky.” For most scholars, that line anchored the painting to the mid-June sky.

In 1984, art historian Albert Boime argued the painting likely shows the dawn of June 19, with the constellation Aries at its center. Two years later, astronomer Charles Whitney used weather records to rule out the 19th and suggested the sky was actually a composite. With the two theories at odds, the idea that Van Gogh painted real stars faded to what the authors call a “marginal minority view.” Most scholars granted only that the bright point near the horizon was likely Venus.

Kim’s team thought the stars were most likely real, and that the early studies were incomplete. No one had tested the painter’s field of view, the shape of its pattern of stars, and the historical record together. Plus, on the morning of June 19, the Moon was a waning gibbous, past half and nothing like the slim crescent depicted on the canvas.

Rather than assuming a date, the researchers asked whether the sky Van Gogh painted could occur, and when. Their method has two core steps. First, they compare a painted sky with astronomical simulations to see whether its arrangement is even possible. Then, they use a statistical method that measures the similarity between two shapes — called Procrustes analysis — to see how faithfully the painting’s star patterns matched real ones. When more historical data is available, the team also works to reconstruct the artist’s field of view from the landscape and check candidate dates against weather records and letters.

As a proof of concept, they applied the method to a handful of other night paintings. A 2011 study had argued that the stars in German painter Adam Elsheimer’s 1609 The Flight into Egypt weren’t copied from a single real sky, and the team’s test agreed. The painting’s Big Dipper sits at an angle to the Milky Way that never occurs in the night sky.

French painter Jean-François Millet’s Starry Night, painted between 1850 and 1865, has no letters, weather records, or known painting site to draw on, so the team ran only the core astronomical tests. The painting’s depiction of Orion — including the bright star Rigel — and Sirius revealed the tightest fit of any painting in the study to an actual star pattern.

Van Gogh’s own 1888 Starry Night Over the Rhône and Café Terrace at Night were next. Because their painting sites in Arles still stand and his letters and local weather records survive, they got the full treatment. The Big Dipper in Starry Night Over the Rhône matched the real sky with what the authors call “relative precision,” and the Summer Triangle in Café Terrace at Night fit even more closely.

RELATED: How Galileo blended science and art before his death 380 years ago

Then came The Starry Night. Van Gogh is thought to have based the painting’s village on an earlier sketch he had completed of Saint-Rémy. After locating a ridge near the asylum whose view matches the drawing, the team found the sketch captures a zoomed-in slice of the landscape. Measured diagonally, from one corner of the frame to the opposite corner, the field of view spans about 18°. They then made an assumption: that Van Gogh likely framed the painting’s night sky the same way. If he did, there should be a morning on record when the Moon, Venus, and a recognizable star pattern all fit inside that narrow frame and are arranged as painted.

Only three dawns in June and July 1889 put the Moon above Venus, as in the painting, inside that field of view: June 23, July 22, and July 23. Only the July mornings also placed the Hyades in view, and on July 22, the Moon and Venus sat in opposite corners with the cluster in between.

At the center of the canvas, a cluster of stars forms a lopsided V that closely resembles the shape of the Hyades cluster. But any handful of dots can coincidentally look like a V. The team had to evaluate whether these stars were intentionally painted as the Hyades or just happened to resemble the cluster’s shape.

They reasoned that if Van Gogh had painted the real cluster, each painted star should have a real counterpart in the same spot. That is, the star at the point of the painted V should match the star at the point of the real V, and so on down each arm of the cluster. Matching them up that way should give a better fit than any other possible combination. So the team tried every possible way of pairing the five painted stars with the five real ones — 120 combinations in all — and measured how well each fit. The natural, point-to-point pairing was the most accurate.

The match was close, but it wasn’t perfect. Of all the paintings the team tested, The Starry Night fit its stars with the lowest statistical accuracy. The authors also acknowledge that a close shape match alone can’t identify a star group. Some other constellations score as well or better and are ruled out only by other evidence, such as where they sat in the sky that morning. Nevertheless, the combined evidence still suggested July 22 as the most likely date.

One objection to this scenario remained. Historians have long held that a severe seizure in mid-July left Van Gogh unable to work. But after rereading his letters alongside weather records from that summer, the team argues the seizure may have struck later, in the last week of July, leaving Van Gogh free to watch the dawn sky on July 22.

The authors argue that the fact that so many clues converge on one morning makes it “hard to reconcile with the premise that the sky on the canvas is an arbitrary creation.” The simpler explanation is that the painting’s sky is rooted in actual observations of the sky on that morning in July — and the burden of proof now falls on anyone who says Van Gogh invented it.

Because the core test needs nothing but the star positions, the team argues it could be turned on other night paintings, including many no one has yet examined. “I hope this work prompts a renewed, multidisciplinary look at the sequence and working methods of the Saint-Rémy paintings, and at Van Gogh’s movements that summer,” Kim said in a statement. “Our conversation with Van Gogh’s other skies is still ongoing. We plan to tell those stories one by one.”

Stellarium, the free planetarium software the team used to cross-check its simulations, lets you simulate the night sky from any place and date. Enter Saint-Rémy-de-Provence, France, and set the clock to a couple hours before sunrise on July 22, 1889. If the sky looks too bright, turn off the atmosphere in the view settings (or press the keyboard shortcut, A).

Then search for the Hyades cluster. Brilliant Venus sits on one side, a slim crescent Moon slightly higher on the other, and between them the V of the Hyades. You can scroll through the hours to watch the scene wheel up from the horizon before dawn. Then step back a day to July 21, or ahead to July 23 and 24, and watch the Moon slide toward Venus. Or compare the view with June 19, the date Boime proposed, and judge for yourself which sky best fits the canvas.

Brooks Mendenhall is a staff writer at Astronomy, based in Chattanooga, Tennessee, fueled by an unending curiosity about the universe. A former classroom teacher, he has a knack for breaking down complex concepts for a wide audience.

Ripple has had one of its busiest stretches in years. It won a national trust bank charter, launched new partnerships and talked up XRP throughout Ripple Swell. Yet XRP is still trading near $1.27. Billy Trajcevski, Head of Strategic Partnerships at UpTrade, says the reason has more to do with Rippleâs structure than with the news itself.

Many holders worry that RLUSD, Rippleâs dollar stablecoin, is taking attention away from XRP. Trajcevski rejected that idea.

âXRP is not a stablecoin. RLUSD is. Itâs really as simple as that,â Trajcevski said.

Trajcevski argued that if XRP competed with RLUSD as a dollar substitute, it would simply sit at $1 forever, which no holder wants. âIf XRP replaces the dollar, weâre all screwed because the price is going to remain flat,â Trajcevski said.

According to Trajcevski, the two assets have different jobs:

A business paying an international invoice in another currency needs a float of XRP to do it, Trajcevski said, and RLUSD alone cannot do that job.

Asked why the good news has not shown up in XRPâs price, Trajcevski pointed to a basic business reality. Ripple is a private company that answers to its shareholders, and shareholders want to see several sources of revenue, not a business that depends on one asset.

âIf your entire business model is based on the success of the XRP token, the board will not be happy, shareholders will not be happy,â Trajcevski said.

That is the message many XRP holders do not want to hear. Ripple will keep building use cases and demand for XRP because that is in its interest, Trajcevski said, but it will not stake its whole business on the token.

Trajcevski also described how Rippleâs public stance toward XRP has changed. During the SEC lawsuit, Ripple kept its distance from the token, as any company in its position would. Now that the case is resolved, Trajcevski said, the company has âcome full circle,â and open support for XRP is back.

The clearest result from Swell, in Trajcevskiâs view, is that XRP has the full backing of the Ripple team.

Trajcevski picked out one headline from Swell that drew little attention: BNY, the worldâs largest custodian, now offers custody for XRP.

BNY serves major institutions, not retail investors. If it is offering XRP custody, Trajcevski said, there is clear institutional demand for the token, or Ripple has made it clear that demand is coming. Trajcevski called it the most bullish news of the event, even though it looked the least important.

Trajcevski also pointed to a session with Bullish CEO Tom Farley. Bullish already uses RLUSD in some of its products, and according to Trajcevski, the exchange is now in active talks with Ripple about new ways to use XRP that do not exist yet.

French Hill’s race still too close to call as House flips, changing the direction of crypto legislation

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Rep. Hill has led the House Financial Services Committee for the past two years, during which members passed the first federal framework for stablecoins as well as a sweeping crypto bill.

As of Wednesday afternoon, House Financial Services Committee Chair French Hill's race for reelection is too close to call as a Democratic takeover in the House is set to usher in a new slate of leaders and reshape the future of crypto legislation in Congress.

On Tuesday night into Wednesday, Hill has been neck and neck with Democratic candidate Chris Jones in Arkansas, according to the Associated Press, with 99% of votes already reported. Hill held a slim lead at publication time.

Hill has led the House Financial Services Committee for the past two years, during which members passed the first federal framework for stablecoins as well as a sweeping crypto bill. The latter stalled in the Senate in September. Crypto political action committee Fairshake and other PACs spent $1 million on Hill's campaign, according to reporting from the Arkansas Democrat Gazette.

Other crypto-friendly lawmakers also secured reelection, including Josh Gottheimer, D-N.J., Rep. Warren Davidson, R-Ohio, Rep. Ritchie Torres, D-N.Y., and current House Agriculture Committee Chair Glenn "GT" Thompson, R-Pa. Later in the day on Wednesday, Rep. Bill Huizenga, R-MI., who is viewed favorably by the crypto industry, lost his seat to Democratic candidate Sean McCann, according to the Associated Press.

Candidate Shomari Figures beat Republican candidate Rhett Marques in Alabama. Figures was endorsed by crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto, given his vote on sweeping digital asset legislation called the Clarity Act.

With the House flipping control, Democrats will usher in the agenda for key congressional committees that have jurisdiction over crypto and prediction markets. Top Democrat Maxine Waters will likely take back her spot as House Financial Services Committee chair, leaving Hill out of his top spot, but likely to be the top Republican on that committee.

Waters is still expected to take up the spot again as chair, but has since been challenged by Democratic Reps. Sean Casten and Bill Foster. Some Democrats have signaled that they want "generational change," given that Waters is 88 years old and Casten is 54, according to reporting from Politico last month.

Regarding crypto, Waters worked on the stablecoin framework dubbed the GENIUS Act and has shown an openness to writing rules for the industry. However, she has also criticized the industry following the collapse of crypto exchange FTX and, later, President Donald Trump's crypto interests and how that can influence regulation.

Famed Iron Age king was actually a woman

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 4, 2026 7:01 PM EST

Ancient Rome famously invaded Britain in 43 CE, under the direction of the emperor Claudius. But his campaign’s success largely hinged on insider information. Just three years’ earlier, an Iron Age tribe living in present-day southeast England known as the Atrebates ousted their ruler named Verica, who subsequently fled for Rome. Once there, Verica convinced Claudius to move forward with plans to cross the English Channel, and add Britain to the empire’s ever-growing domain. In exchange for restoring his place on the Atrebates’ throne, Verica agreed to provide Roman troops with tactical support and guidance.

In a new discussion published today in the journal Current Archaeology, researchers argue that historians have consistently gotten one key detail incorrect for at least 200 years. Based on their analysis of ancient artifacts and contextual information, the Iron Age ruler Verica was a woman, not a man.

“Verica is assumed to have been male because of the patriarchal mindset of 19th century classical historians,” study co-author and Bournemouth University archaeologist Miles Russell said in a statement. “Verica was not only critical to the special relationship between Britain and Rome in the early first century [CE] but was indisputably female.”

The consequential re-evaluation is based on three major pieces of evidence—the first simply being Verica’s name. Ending in “a” was common for women’s names in Iron Age Britain. Two of Verica’s most recognizable contemporaries, Boudica of the Iceni and Cartimandua of the Brigantes, were both female leaders of their respective tribes. Boudica is particularly famous for leading a failed revolt against the Romans in 60 CE, but Russell and his colleagues argue their revision makes Verica a much more consequential figure.

The second reason relates to Roman accounts of Claudius. Many Roman writers of the era were highly critical of the emperor, and described him as weak or inept. Critics often cited his consultations with women and the formerly enslaved as proof. Despite his success in Britain, Roman historians like Cassius Dio tied his decision to Verica, a woman.

“The story of Claudius committing to something only because a woman told him to, fits the trope of this emperor as being submissive and henpecked,” argued Russell. “In effect, Dio was saying that the campaign was undertaken solely because of the whisperings of a barbarian femme fatale.”

Finally, the archaeologists point to Verica’s own coinage. Once reinstated to her throne, the ruler was allowed to mint her own currency featuring the Latin title “rex” to indicate she enjoyed the emperor’s full support. Many people mistakenly assume rex is only a masculine term, when it was actually applied to any gender.

“Both male and female kings were recognised by Rome, Cleopatra of Egypt being the most famous,” Russell added.

Verica’s image on the coins also reinforces the argument. Other British Iron Age leaders also produced coins, but those portray clearly male fighters. Verica’s currency shows a slender, feminine individual on horseback with bare breasts. Russell argues that taking all of this “at face value” shows Verica was clearly a woman—and a very powerful woman at that.

“Had she not been deposed, fleeing to Claudius for military support, Rome may never have invaded Britain in the first place,” he said.

Bitcoin price slips below $77K – Why post-election BTC crash fears persist

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

The voters have spoken. Now, the focus shifts to how investors react.

The chances of Democrats winning have already increased to 99%, and today’s outcome has seen them sweep both houses of Congress (Senate and House of Representatives). The question now is whether this shift brings some certainty to the market and allows Bitcoin to recover, or will it add another layer of volatility.

According to the latest Santiment data, Bitcoin retail sentiment has become increasingly bearish after the election results. The chart below shows that there are now only 0.51 bullish comments for every bearish comment. With many traders viewing the Republicans as more crypto-friendly, the Democratic sweep has added another layer of uncertainty to an already weak market structure.

Notably, this uncertainty is now showing up across both on-chain and technical indicators.

According to CryptoQuant, Bitcoin’s bounce from recent lows appears to be slowing, with low spot demand and prolonged liquidations taking Bitcoin towards $76,800. Put simply, there is some spot buying, but it isn’t strong enough to offset the selling and liquidation from the futures market. Meanwhile, BTC dominance has faced its third rejection near the 61% range, failing to break higher. This signals fading relative strength.

Together, Bitcoin [BTC] slipping below $77k, weak on-chain momentum, and increasingly bearish retail sentiment are creating a very fragile market structure as investors digest the Democratic sweep. Therefore, is the recent rally in the Bitcoin Fear and Greed Index simply a case of the bears taking a break before sentiment falls into “extreme” fear as the Bitcoin crash narrative takes hold?

Bitcoin’s Fear and Greed Index dropped to 26 for the first time in three months.

There is no doubt that the FUD surrounding elections affected sentiment, but since the election has occurred, the index has recovered over 30%. This shows that due to the Democratic sweep, there is less political uncertainty in the market, which has led to a short-term recovery in sentiment. However, on-chain and technical indicators have yet to show the same recovery.

According to AMBCrypto, this gap between sentiment and market momentum could be important. As noted by one analyst, Bitcoin has historically faced sell-offs after elections, raising the possibility of a 30% correction if the pattern repeats. For now, improving sentiment has kept Bitcoin from sliding into extreme fear; however, clear signs of accumulation are still missing.

This leaves a clear divergence between investor confidence and underlying market demand.

Unless this gap starts to narrow, the latest rebound in Bitcoin’s Fear and Greed Index may just be a short-term bounce after the elections. So, while the Democratic sweep may have taken away some of the political uncertainty, it has yet to change Bitcoin’s underlying market structure.

In this context, another midterm-driven sell-off cannot be ruled out.

Bitcoin’s weak demand, falling dominance, and bearish retail sentiment point to continued market pressure despite the recent sentiment recovery.

The Fear and Greed Index has bounced over 30%, but buying strength remains weak, leaving room for another Bitcoin sell-off.

This self-powered toothbrush makes electricity to kill cavity-causing bacteria

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Researchers have created a self-charging toothbrush that helps fight the bacteria linked to cavities and gum disease while preventing plaque buildup.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A toothbrush that generates its own electricity with every stroke could offer a new way to fight the microbes behind cavities and gum disease.

Conventional toothbrushes rely on physical scrubbing to remove plaque from teeth and gums. But even after a thorough brush, some bacteria remain and begin rebuilding plaque. To solve that problem, researchers have developed a self-powered toothbrush that converts the friction of brushing into an electrical charge. That electricity is intended to zap the plaque-promoting bacteria and keep them from colonizing teeth.

"It's great to have new approaches that actually enhance the effect of mechanical cleaning," said Nicholas Jakubovics, an oral microbiologist at Newcastle University in the U.K. who was not involved in the study. "The idea that the toothbrush surface itself is actively doing something is really interesting."

The experimental brush has bristles made from polyvinylidene fluoride (PVDF), a plastic that generates electricity when bent; these types of energy-producing materials are called piezoelectric. As the bristles flex during brushing, they produce an electrical charge that then triggers chemical reactions in saliva and water. These reactions release reactive oxygen species (ROS), which are highly reactive molecules that can damage bacterial cells.

Even when the bristles are not being used to brush, they carry a faint surface charge that helps prevent bacteria from clinging to the brush head when the toothbrush isn't in use, the team reported Wednesday (Nov. 4) in the journal Science Advances.

In laboratory tests where the toothbrush was used on isolated germs, its electrified bristles killed more than half of the Streptococcus mutans bacteria present. These microbes contribute to tooth decay. By comparison, nylon bristles killed just 9% of the bacteria. They also reduced levels of Porphyromonas gingivalis, a bacterium linked to gum disease, by up to 78%, while nylon brushes reduced them by less than 8%.

When the researchers added an antioxidant — a compound that neutralizes ROS — the new toothbrush's antibacterial effect largely disappeared, highlighting that the ROS are likely the key to its effects.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

The team also found that the activity of certain genes in both S. mutans and P. gingivalis fell after exposure to the electrified bristles. These genes are involved in making ATP-binding cassette (ABC) transporters — molecular pumps that help shuttle essential nutrients across bacterial cell membranes. The researchers suspect that disrupting this machinery makes it harder for these microbes to survive and cling to teeth.

The new toothbrush still needs to be tested in further safety trials.

But Jakubovics was not convinced by that part of the team's explanation. "ABC transporters transport all sorts of different things," he said, adding that the findings did not clearly show how changes in these genes affected the bacteria.

The researchers also tested the new brush against plaque biofilms — hardy communities of bacteria that stick together and cling to tooth surfaces. For the experiment, they grew human dental plaque on cow tooth enamel and found that the brush removed more than 99% of the biofilm, outperforming a regular nylon brush.

Finally, the team moved to humans, putting the brush to the test in 115 adults with gum inflammation or tooth decay. These study participants were randomly assigned to use either the new toothbrushes or traditional nylon-bristled brushes twice a day for three months. (The study didn't note whether participants noticed a difference in sensation when using the electrified brushes.)

By the end of the trial, the participants who used the electrified bristles had less plaque, less gum inflammation and less bleeding than the participants who used ordinary brushes. Plaque samples also showed that the people who used electrified brushes had lower levels of bacteria associated with oral disease and higher levels of beneficial bacteria, which have previously been reported to suppress harmful oral bacteria and support oral health.

The researchers think that the disease-linked bacteria may have relatively fragile cell membranes, making them more vulnerable to damage from ROS than the beneficial bacteria. That's a theory that requires further study, though, so it's not completely clear how selective the brush is against plaque-causing bacteria.

In a test of the toothbrush's durability, the PVDF bristles retained their structure and electrical properties over three months of simulated use. Lab-grown mouse cells repeatedly exposed to brush material showed no signs of damage, and brushing on cow tooth enamel did not increase surface roughness. However, these tests cannot establish whether repeated use of these brushes is safe for humans.

"We would want to make sure that it is safe on the tissues for long-term use," Jakubovics said. He noted that, in lab tests, bacteria are grown directly on the bristles and exposed to their electricity for hours, "whereas in reality, in the mouth, your bristle is only in contact with the tooth or oral tissue for a very short time."

The trial with humans provides some insight into how the brush works in real life, but it was relatively small. Larger studies will be needed to find out whether the toothbrush offers a meaningful advantage over conventional brushes. For now, Jakubovics sees the recent study as just a proof of concept; more information and more convincing clinical data are still needed, he emphasized.

This article is for informational purposes only and is not meant to offer medical advice.

Lu, Y., Song, J., Chi, X., Li, W., Wu, L., Guo, Y., Cui, Q., Zheng, X., Bai, Y., Heng, B. C., Han, X., Zhang, X., & Deng, X. (2026). Self-charged toothbrush facilitates tooth healthcare maintenance. Science Advances, 12, Article eaeg5744. https://doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.aeg5744

Isha Ishtiaq is a freelance health journalist based in Pakistan who covers health, medicine and biomedical research. Her work has appeared in Live Science, Science News, and Medscape, among other outlets. She holds a B.S. (Hons) in biotechnology from the University of Gujrat and an M.S. in biological sciences from the University of Sialkot, where her research focused on the use of deep learning models for disease detection.
