British healthcare worker with Ebola evacuated from DR Congo to UK

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Patient is ⁠in ​stable condition and will be transferred to a specialist ‘high consequence’ infectious disease unit

A British healthcare worker with Ebola is being evacuated from the Democratic Republic of the Congo to the UK for medical treatment, it has been confirmed.

“A British citizen currently in the DR Congo has tested positive for Ebola,” a spokesperson for the UK government said in a statement on Wednesday. “The UK has well-established procedures for dealing with cases like this, and those processes have been activated … The risk to the UK public from Ebola remains low.”

The patient, whose identity is unknown, is in a stable condition and is expected to arrive in the UK on Wednesday or Thursday before being transferred in a specialist ambulance to a “high consequence” infectious disease unit, according to an official document seen by Reuters.

A humanitarian organisation sent the worker to DR Congo to help in the response to the outbreak, which is the second largest on record. It is the first case involving a British national contracting Ebola in the current outbreak.

The Royal Free hospital in London has a high-level isolation unit for infectious diseases such as Ebola; other centres are in Newcastle and Liverpool.

Ebola is a highly contagious, severe and often fatal illness. It is spread through direct contact with body fluids such as the blood or vomit of infected people, or dead bodies.

The current outbreak, declared on 15 May by DR Congo’s health ministry, has surpassed 10,000 cases, and 5,000 deaths. It is the 17th in DR Congo since Ebola was identified, was caused by the Bundibugyo virus, and has become the largest and deadliest in the country’s history. The Bundibugyo virus has no approved treatment, though vaccine trials are under way.

Since the outbreak started, it has spread to Kenya and Uganda in east Africa, while last month Nigeria confirmed a case in the capital, Abuja, after a woman who arrived from Uganda tested positive for the virus. She, along with a close contact, were evacuated to Switzerland for treatment.

In an interview with the Guardian last month, Dr Jean Kaseya, the director general of the Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), said the “unprecedented” spread of the Ebola outbreak meant it could reach the UK and other countries.

“We want all countries to know that they are not safe if they don’t take appropriate measures,” he said. “Each country, not only in the African region, but all continents, must now increase the preparedness level and the possibility for treatment.

“It is the first time three African regions have been affected [by Ebola]. The geographic extension of this outbreak is unprecedented.”

Other patients who have been medically evacuated for specialised care since the start of the outbreak include two sent to Germany and one to the Netherlands. Another case was reported in France in July.

British health authorities say the risk ⁠to the wider public from imported Ebola cases remains low and the NHS has procedures in place to manage suspected and confirmed infections.

There have only been four confirmed cases recorded in Britain since 1976, ‌according to the UK Health Security Agency.

10 Foreign True Crime Documentaries To Scratch Two Itches At Once

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Travel the world or curl up on the couch to watch true crime documentaries? Why not do both?

Travel the world or curl up on the couch to watch true crime documentaries? It’s the universal conundrum. On the one hand, the world is a big and beautiful place full of opportunities to eat delicious food and meet new people and wear hats you would never wear at home. On the other hand, there are true crime stories that need to be solved by you in your pajamas on the couch under a weighted blanket.

How do you scratch both of those itches? Well, you could travel the world seeking out true crimes to solve yourself, but that would get pricey and probably pretty dangerous. You don’t want to become the subject of your own true crime documentary, after all. Instead, settle in on a long, rainy weekend (or when it’s bright and sunny, I’m not judging) to watch these engrossing true crime documentaries set in beautiful locations around the world. It’s safer and the snacks are cheaper.

In 1996, French film producer Sophie Toscan du Plantier was found murdered outside her remote holiday cottage in the small village of Toormore. West Cork two days before Christmas. Sophie was just 39 years old and mom to son Pierre-Louis. A local journalist was one of the first to report her murder, and quickly became the main suspect. Netflix’s three-part docuseries Sophie - A Murder in West Cork covers not only Sophie’s tragic death, but also the struggle between Ireland and France to convict the killer.

Amy Lynn Bradley was just 23 years old when she went missing from the Royal Caribbean cruise ship Rhapsody of the Sea one early morning in March 1998. The ship was en route to the island of Curaçao. Her room showed no sign of a struggle, and several items were left behind. Authorities initially considered Bradley’s disappearance to be a potential accident or suicide, but Netflix’s 2025 three-part docuseries Amy Bradley is Missing speculates on several other possibilities that could have happened in the sunny tropics.

We do not love a scam artist. Especially one who preys on women looking for love, as Israeli conman Shimon Hayut aka Simon Leviev did between 2017 to 2019. Hayut swindled millions of dollars from women in the Netherlands, Norway, and other countries around Europe, leaving them destitute and broken-hearted. That’s the bad news. The good news is the way these women eventually found each other, banding together to bring Hayut to justice and try to reclaim their money. Netflix’s 2022 documentary The Tinder Swindler tells the story of three women who lost nearly everything to Hayut, and how they came together to bring him down.

The Netflix 2026 three-part docuseries Should I Marry a Murderer? will stay with you for a long time after the fact. Not just because it’s set in the gorgeous Scottish countryside, but because of the perspective it offers on what it might actually look like to find out the man you love is genuinely a murderer. Caroline Muirhead met handsome Scottish farmer Alexander “Sandy” McKellar on Tinder, and the two got engaged in 2020 after a whirlwind four month romance. When McKellar confessed to a murder that happened three years earlier, Muirhead stays with him. Told from her modern day perspective, Should I Marry a Murderer? Is an unflinching glimpse at what we might be willing to overlook in the name of love.

Vittorio Emanuele was nine years old when his family was exiled from Italy in 1946. His father was King Umberto II, and Vittorio was meant to be a prince until Italy voted to create a republic without the royal family. Living a life of luxurious exile, Vittorio was on the French island of Corsica in 1978 when he was involved in the death of 19-year-old German tourist Dirk Hamer. Netflix’s 2023 three-part series The King Who Never Was features interviews with Vittorio himself, as well as Dirk Hamer’s family members who were determined to see the privileged deposed royal charged with Dirk’s murder.

Michel Fourniret confessed to killing 11 women and one man in the picturesque Ardennes region of France between 1987 to 2003, making him one of the most notorious serial killers in modern French history. The Belgian killer only confessed after his seemingly submissive wife, Monique Olivier, provided police with enough information to force his hand. Which meant, of course, that she knew something of her husband’s crimes. More than police might have suspected, if the 2023 Netflix limited series Monique Olivier: Accessory to Evil proves anything. Olivier might have even been a willing participant in her husband’s horrific crimes. Chilling.

Fair warning for the 2019 five-part Netflix docuseries Who Killed Little Gregory? - the entire series revolves around the awful kidnapping and murder of 4-year-old Grégory Villemin in a small village in eastern France. Grégory’s murder has never been solved, and this docuseries delves into the disturbing allegations that members of his own extended family might have murdered the little boy. This one is equal parts difficult to watch and completely engrossing.

If you’re looking for some real-life Scandinavian noir to get you through the dark nights of fall, you’ll want to check out The Lørenskog Disappearance, Netflix’s five-part docuseries based on the true story of real life Norwegian billionaire Tom Hagan. Hagan’s wife Anne-Elisabeth Hagan went missing from the couple’s opulent home in October 2018, and a ransom note asking for nine million euros was left in her wake. The police originally believed Anne-Elisabeth was kidnapped, but began to suspect murder when the alleged kidnappers didn’t continue to reach out. While this 2022 limited series is a drama and not a straight documentary, it really delivers on Scandi vibes and billionaire intrigue.

British tourist Grace Millane was taking a gap year to travel the world when she ended up in New Zealand in 2018. She planned to head back home to her family for Christmas. She never made it back home. Millane was reported missing by her family, with police conducting a massive search in Auckland and the surrounding area before zeroing in on Jesse Kempson, her final Tinder date. The 2024 documentary The Lie: The Murder of Grace Millane on Amazon Prime, features interview tapes with Kempson as he is interrogated by police, and his subsequent attempt to use the so-called “rough sex murder” defense for killing the 22-year-old. On her birthday.

If you’ve been thinking of spending a bit of time in the UK along the coast, the Apple TV series Coastal Killers will either intrigue you or scare you off, depending on your perspective. Instead of focusing on just one true crime story, Coastal Killers has eight seasons of stories of mayhem in lovely little seaside towns in the UK...

...Which should keep you busy as you plan your next potential trip.

Why Bitter Coffee Starts to Taste So Good to Your Brain

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A neurobiology investigation led by researchers at UC San Francisco, Yale University, and the Max Planck Institute reveals how the brain transforms unpalatable tastes into beloved foods over time. Published in Nature, the study maps an internal, “slow” dopamine circuit connecting the digestive tract to the anterior basolateral amygdala (BLA).

Unlike fast dopamine bursts triggered by immediate external cues, this gut-derived signal builds over minutes as nutrients arrive in the gastrointestinal tract, selectively activating the left BLA in both mice and humans. This sustained signaling enables animals to link initially adverse flavors, such as the bitterness of coffee, with post-ingestive caloric rewards, establishing how dietary preferences are conditioned and potentially modified.

A person’s very first sip of black coffee, dark chocolate, or tonic water is rarely enjoyable. Bitter gustatory cues serve as an evolutionarily conserved warning system designed to deter animals from ingesting plant toxins and spoiled organic matter.

Yet, millions of adults develop fierce cravings for these exact bitter profiles. How the mammalian nervous system learns to overcome innate taste aversions and develop robust preferences for initially unappealing foods has long puzzled sensory and metabolic neuroscientists.

Scientists have established that dopamine reinforces pursuit behaviors. However, the overwhelming majority of addiction and feeding research has focused on “fast” dopamine transmission: millisecond-level spikes generated in the striatum when an animal perceives external sensory stimuli, such as the sight, aroma, or tongue contact with a sugary treat.

Now, a collaborative study published November 4, 2026, in Nature identifies an understudied “slow” dopamine pathway that monitors visceral signals from within the digestive tract, actively remodeling flavor preferences based on post-ingestive caloric feedback.

Led by Dr. Zachary Knight, PhD, professor of physiology and investigator at the Howard Hughes Medical Institute at UCSF, alongside former doctoral student Dr. James Grove, PhD, the researchers sought to isolate post-ingestive reward from immediate oral sensations.

To do this, the team infused nutrients, either sugar solutions or calorie-dense, milkshake-like lipid emulsions, directly into the stomachs of mice via gastric catheters while tracking real-time dopamine release across diverse subcortical regions.

Bypassing oral taste receptors revealed an unsuspected neurochemical signature:

“We’ve shown that this slow dopamine system is what drives us to learn to like certain foods over time, and we can see where and how that happens,” explains Dr. Knight. “Once your brain has learned to associate the flavor of your favorite brew with the uplift from caffeine, that coffee starts to taste a lot better.”

To determine whether this slow BLA dopamine wave directly dictates food learning, the team performed loss- and gain-of-function behavioral paradigms:

Collaborating with clinical neuroimaging groups, the team examined whether this visceral dopamine mechanism operates in the human central nervous system.

Yale University psychologist Dr. Dana Small, PhD, re-evaluated positron emission tomography (PET) datasets from adult men consuming caloric versus non-caloric beverages, finding that dopamine release concentrated predominantly within the left BLA once nutrients entered the gastrointestinal tract. Concurrently, Dr. Marc Tittgemeyer, PhD, and colleagues at the Max Planck Institute for Metabolism Research deployed functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) in 38 adults, demonstrating elevated blood-oxygen-level-dependent (BOLD) activation localized to the identical left-hemispheric amygdalar coordinates following glucose ingestion.

In both mice and humans, this nutrient-evoked dopamine release exhibited marked left-sided lateralization over the right hemisphere. This asymmetry was unexpected, as subcortical feeding circuits governing primary survival drives are traditionally considered bilateral and symmetrical. Investigating why evolution partitioned this visceral conditioning system to the left hemisphere remains an active area of investigation.

Deciphering the gut-to-amygdala dopamine circuit introduces novel targets for treating metabolic dysfunction, compulsive overeating, and obesity.

Current pharmaceutical breakthroughs, such as glucagon-like peptide-1 (GLP-1) receptor agonists, dramatically suppress appetite, yet their central neurochemical interplay with nutrient reinforcement pathways is not completely understood. Knight speculates that GLP-1 receptor activation may dampen nutrient-evoked dopamine signaling in the BLA, reducing the hedonic reinforcement of calorie-dense diets.

Unraveling the molecular checkpoints connecting enteric nutrient sensors to subcortical learning circuits provides an anatomical framework to not just suppress hunger, but potentially recalibrate dietary preferences toward healthier foods.

How does the “slow” dopamine pathway differ from traditional reward signaling?

Traditional dopamine research centers on “fast” signaling, rapid bursts occurring within fractions of a second when an animal sees, smells, or tastes food. The “slow” pathway originates from nutrient sensors in the gastrointestinal tract, releasing dopamine into the anterior basolateral amygdala over several minutes after ingestion to link internal nutritional benefits with flavor memories.

Where in the brain does this gut-driven learning take place?

The signal concentrates in the anterior basolateral amygdala (BLA). In both mice and humans (verified via PET and fMRI imaging), nutrient-driven dopamine release exhibits a strong lateralization toward the left BLA compared to the right hemisphere.

Could this pathway explain how GLP-1 obesity drugs alter food cravings?

While GLP-1 receptor agonists were not tested directly in this investigation, researchers hypothesize that these gut-hormone mimics may suppress this nutrient-evoked dopamine signaling in the amygdala, diminishing the subconscious reinforcement that makes high-fat and high-sugar foods desirable over time.

Funding: This work was supported by the National Institutes of Health (grants R01-DK106399, R01-DK138127, R01-NS116626, and R01-DK145100 and F31-NS120468).

Disclosures: The authors declare no competing interests.

The neural basis of flavor-nutrient learning

Animals learn about the external world in part through interoceptive signals1,2. For example, the nutrient content of food is first estimated in the mouth, in the form of flavour, and then it is measured again through slower signals from the gut. How these signals from the mouth and gut are integrated to drive learning is unclear.

Here we identify a dopamine (DA) pathway that enables learning about the nutrient content of food. We show that DA neurons in the ventral tegmental area are necessary for associating nutrients with flavours, and that post-ingestive nutrients trigger DA release selectively in a small region of the anterior basolateral amygdala (BLA) but not in canonical DA targets in striatum.

This nutrient-triggered DA release occurs preferentially in the left BLA in mice, and this same structure is preferentially activated by post-ingestive nutrients in humans. We identify the gut sensors that are responsible for nutrient-triggered DA release; show that they activate BLA-projecting DA neurons defined by expression of cholecystokinin; and demonstrate that stimulation of DA axon terminals in the anterior BLA drives flavour–nutrient learning but not other aspects of feeding behaviour.

Two-photon imaging of neurons in the left anterior BLA reveals that they integrate gustatory and post-ingestive cues, and silencing these neurons prevents flavour–nutrient learning.

These findings establish a neural basis for how animals learn about the nutrient content of their food.

Washington voters keep millionaires tax, splitting tech leaders over the state’s competitive edge

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop, Lisa Stiffler & Kurt Schlosser on Nov 4, 2026 at 10:10 amNovember 4, 2026 at 10:18 am

Washington voters appear to have upheld the state’s new tax on high earners, putting the state on track to lose its place among the nine states without an income tax — a status that tech leaders have long touted as an edge in recruiting companies and talent.

Initiative 645, which would repeal the 9.9% tax on household income above $1 million, was trailing 58% to 42% in election-night returns. King County voters rejected the repeal by an even wider margin, with 66.5% voting no. The repeal would need about 62% of the remaining ballots to pass, according to a Seattle Times estimate.

The tax has divided the Seattle tech community since lawmakers unveiled the proposal in February. Some founders and investors warned it would drive talent and capital out of the state, while others called those fears overblown.

Madrona, the Seattle venture capital firm, gave $250,000 to the repeal campaign, making it one of the biggest donors.

“The outcome on the income tax should be disappointing for all Washingtonians as having no income tax was a major source of competitive advantage for the state,” said Matt McIlwain, a Madrona managing director, responding to questions from GeekWire.

McIlwain, who urged voters to repeal the tax in a GeekWire op-ed in October, said the state saw record economic growth and job creation after voters rejected an income tax in 2010. “I am concerned for the future ability of Washington state to be competitive,” he wrote.

Ben Golden, an attorney and advisor to Pacific Northwest startups and investors, countered that the election outcome demonstrates “that Washingtonians are ready to invest in ourselves.”

“There’s lots of hard work to follow to ensure that revenue is spent wisely, but at least (Washington) will be ready for the wave of Medicaid cuts and AI job disruption on the horizon,” added Golden, who in February penned an op-ed in support of the tax.

And while some residents will leave for tax havens, Golden said, “the opportunity remains ripe for the investor community and startups to build and grow in this healthy and talent-packed region.”

Brian Fioca, a Seattle-based serial entrepreneur and investor, hopes the additional tax dollars will improve the quality of life in the state overall.

“Washington is a wonderful place to live and build a company, full of extraordinary people and talent,” said Fioca, who also offered an earlier op-ed on the issue. “If someone decides not to start a business here, or chooses to leave because of this tax, that’s not someone I’d be particularly excited to work with or invest in anyway.”

Asked whether the outcome changes how Madrona thinks about investing in Washington, McIlwain didn’t address the state specifically. “Our goal remains to find and partner with amazing founders and their teams to build enduring companies that create innovative products, jobs and economic returns,” he said.

State Sen. Jamie Pedersen, D-Seattle, was an original architect of the tax and the prime sponsor of the bill that created it. As Senate majority leader, he kept reworking the measure to win votes until the final days of this year’s legislative session. Pedersen, who has been in the Legislature since 2007, is also executive vice president and general counsel at Seattle construction and engineering firm McKinstry.

At the No on 645 campaign’s election-night event in Seattle on Tuesday, Pedersen celebrated the outcome.

“We have been saddled with an anchor around our neck, with a tax system that is bad for working people, that’s bad for small businesses, that’s bad for just about everybody except for the one percent,” he said, as seen in a video posted on Instagram.

The No on 645 campaign touted a coalition of working families, labor unions, teachers, small business owners and others — and attracted endorsements from state celebrities like Rick Steves, the Edmonds-based travel writer, and “Jeopardy!” host Ken Jennings.

The tax applies only to income above the $1 million threshold, so a household earning $1.5 million would owe $49,500. It takes effect on 2028 income, with the first payments due in April 2029.

Jeff Bezos, Howard Schultz and Rich Barton have all moved out of state in recent years, though each described the move in personal terms.

McIlwain, who moved with his family from Georgia to Seattle 26 years ago, became a Georgia resident again two years ago. That was before lawmakers unveiled the millionaires tax in February.

In 2010, Steve Ballmer and Microsoft helped fund the campaign that defeated a statewide income tax, but the region’s biggest tech companies stayed out of this fight. The state projects the tax will raise $3 billion to $4 billion a year from roughly 25,000 households. Gov. Bob Ferguson signed it into law in March, along with tax breaks for small businesses and lower-income families.

A lawsuit filed in April by former Attorney General Rob McKenna and former state Supreme Court Justice Phil Talmadge argues the tax is unconstitutional, citing a 1933 ruling that classified income as property, which the state constitution says must be taxed at a uniform rate.

The case will likely go to the state Supreme Court, where Democrat-backed candidates led four of five races on Tuesday’s ballot as of election-night returns, according to the Washington State Standard.

Bread as a Sponge for Oil Is the Best Kitchen Hack I Learned All Year

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Oily pans, stews and sauces are no match for the bread sponge trick.

If you want to degrease a pan or the top layer of an oily sauce without ruining a sponge, a single slice of bread could be your new favorite cleaning sidekick.

While we have vastly improved how we dispose of most things, excess cooking grease still presents a problem. Down the sink? Not advisable. Neither is the dishwasher—even the garbage bin can become a gnarly affair.

Getting the bulk of the grease out of a pan after cooking a batch of bacon or burgers is a good first step, but that greasy pan will either take down a sponge or half a roll of paper towels. What if a humble slice of past-its-prime sandwich bread was the all-natural solution for greasy pans you’ve been looking for?

It’s cheap and easy, and you can toss the used-up bread into a compost bin or countertop food waste processor without disrupting its yuck-free operation.

So, is it ever OK to pour excess grease or used cooking oil down the sink? Even if you run the hot water tap at the same time? Short answer: no. Long answer? Here’s an expert for the “no.” “When grease cools, it solidifies, coating the interior of the pipe,” says Jacylyn Basilone, chief operating officer of Brooks Plumbing. Even if you’re running hot water, the grease will eventually cool further down the line and cause future problems. “Over time, the solidified grease in the pipes can catch debris, thus making a partial clog grow larger and larger,” she says, reducing the pipe’s circumference little by little if you do this repeatedly.

Unlike a clogged shower drain, which you can usually clear easily, “a normal sewer machine or ‘snake’ can’t alleviate this type of stoppage,” says Basilone. “While grease solidifies, it can’t be captured by the claw head. Grease removal is best remediated with a hydrojetter, which tends to be more expensive than the traditional drain-cleaning services.”

Bread is the real answer, and I can’t believe I haven’t been doing this all along. (*Hangs head in shame, fully guilty of relying on the obviously very sketchy hot water method.*)

Bread has always been a superb grease-mopping tool; we usually think of it in a culinary context rather than for waste removal. Just as you’d drag a crusty baguette or hunk of ciabatta across the drippings on your plate, bread is an excellent sponge for all manner of oil, whether or not it is appealing to consume. Using bread to soak up excess oil essentially turns liquid food waste into a solid, making disposal much easier.

If it’s not obvious, this is also a great way to use bread that might otherwise go to waste: the ends of the loaf you can’t bring yourself to eat, or bread that’s gone stale or is starting to show the first mold spores. If you’re going to discard the bread anyway, at least put it to good use.

The bread trick comes in handy in almost endless ways. If there’s a pool of oil, it’s the mop for the job. I initially tried it after cooking bacon. A single, small slice of bread was enough to absorb what was easily four to six ounces of liquid grease left in the pan. I dragged the slice around the pan like a mop, but even after letting it sit a few extra moments, it kept working and eventually soaked up all the liquid grease. (If you’re feeling indulgent and used a fresh bread slice for this purpose, the temptation to make pretty epic croutons here is quite strong — another upside.)

If your air fryer has space between the drawer and the basket, you can place the bread there to soak up grease while cooking. Even for oily, slow-cooked proteins like pork shoulder, you can press the bread onto the meat to soak up grease before serving.

Once the bread has absorbed the oil and needs discarding, responsible food-waste methods are much easier to manage. Depending on how much oil the bread has absorbed, you can safely put the soaked bread in the compost bin or countertop composter without risking a major oil spill and a mess to clean up later. Oil can slow decomposition, so composting large amounts isn’t recommended. Small amounts, such as a few tablespoons at a time, are safe.

Even if you have no other option but to throw it away — and if that is the case, please consider getting a small composting appliance — at the very least, you’re not involving an easily recyclable glass container.

For more home tips, learn this neat trick for reusing frying oil and learn the 10 worst things to pour down your drain.

Canon Announces Timer Remotes for Astrophotographers and a Compact Hotshoe Mic

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Canon Announces Timer Remotes for Astrophotographers and a Compact Hotshoe Mic

Alongside new Cinema EOS firmware, Canon has announced some new accessories for its mirrorless EOS R System cameras, including timer remote controllers and a hotshoe microphone.

The new Timer Remote Controllers, the TC-N3 and TC-E3, build upon the legacy of Canon’s Timer Remote Controller TC-80N3 for film and digital SLR cameras. Timer Remote Controllers are designed for specialized photographic applications, including precisely capturing long-exposure photos in bulb mode and automating and customizing long-interval photography for things like star trails and timelapses.

Compared to the TC-80N3, the new TC-N3 and TC-E3 controllers feature a 20% larger LCD panel with bigger numbers and icons, a new orange illumination backlight for better visibility at night, a new clock function to schedule shooting sessions, and tripod compatibility. It still includes dedicated shooting controls, and the remotes run on AAA batteries.

The Timer Remote Controller TC-N3 is compatible with the Canon EOS R1, EOS R3, EOS R5, EOS R5 Mark II, and EOS R5 C, and other cameras with an N3 remote terminal.

The TC-E3 works with cameras with an E3 terminal, including the EOS C50, EOS R6 Mark III, EOS R6 V, EOS R6 Mark II, EOS R8 Mark II, EOS R8, EOS R7, EOS R10, EOS R50 V, and EOS R100.

“The remotes offer photographers great flexibility and precision when capturing landscapes, astrophotography, and self-portraits, particularly in low-light environments or when composing a group photo or self-portrait from a distance,” Canon says.  The new remote controllers will begin shipping in early January. The TC-N3 is $189.99 while the TC-E3 is $209.99.

Canon TC-N3Buy new on B&HCanon TC-N3Buy used on KEH.comCanon TC-E3Buy new on B&HCanon TC-E3Buy used on KEH.com

Canon has also announced the DM-E10D, a new monaural digital microphone for use on Canon’s Multi-Function Shoe.

Compatible with a wide range of Canon cameras, including the EOS R1, R3, R5 II, R6 III, R6 II, R8 II, R8, R7, R10, R50 V, R6 V, and more, the DM-E10D features a super cardioid polar pattern mic that prioritizes sound from the front while reducing unwanted noise from the sides. It also picks up sound from the rear, so it can capture conversations between the camera operator and a subject.

The compact on-camera mic features cable-free operation and has a Menu button for easy access to audio settings. It draws power directly from the camera and includes a built-in shock mount and wind cover.

Canon notes that the DM-E10D is its smallest and lightest DM-series microphone to date. It will begin shipping by the end of December and is $209.99.

Canon has also announced new Cropping Guide firmware for the EOS R6 Mark III full-frame mirrorless camera. The firmware update has four different guides, including top and bottom guidelines to fit different head sizes and guidelines for positioning props in the frame.

This firmware is designed for high-volume photographers, like school and sports photographers, to consistently capture photos with the same framing of many subjects in a short time. It also includes color-coded guides for common print sizes, including 5×7 and 8×10 aspect ratios.

Canon will begin selling the EOS R6 III with the Cropping Guide firmware already installed for $2,919 in early December. It will also be available separately as a $120 service charge for existing owners.

'We would have been better off never developing it': The 'Godfather of AI' warns we may regret creating AI

Geoffrey Hinton gives his latest thoughts on the evolving tech

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Geoffrey Hinton’s name has been making the rounds a lot more often nowadays due to his commentary on the very technology his research helped develop.

During an interview with the Financial Times, he predicted who will benefit financially the most from the continued evolution of AI. “It's going to create massive unemployment and a huge rise in profits,” he pointed out. “It will make a few people much richer and most people poorer. That's not AI's fault, that is the capitalist system.”

And during an appearance on The Diary Of A CEO podcast, Hinton gave his thoughts on the topic of AI being misused and the associated risks that come with it. “There's risks that come from people misusing AI, and that's most of the risks and all of the short-term risks,” he noted. “And then there's risks that come from AI getting super smart and understanding it doesn't need us.”

Now he’s back in the news for (once again) giving a stern warning about AI. Here’s what he had to say during his latest interview.

During a conversation with The Guardian, Hinton made it clear that he doesn’t see himself as an AI doomer and that he genuinely believes that AI has the potential to lead to great things happening in the future. But he balanced out that optimistic outlook with a more sobering one when he stated that “it might be that we would have been better off never developing it.”

Hinton also gave a very blunt answer when the discussion moved over to the major leaders backing the companies that keep releasing even more advanced versions of their signature models. “I think [Mark] Zuckerberg and [Elon] Musk and Sam Altman, they understand the risks, and they don’t give a s**t,” he said. “They’d rather have the money. That’s my belief.” As Hinton’s conversation with The Guardian continued, he presented the idea of a federal agency that keeps a close eye on AI and makes sure the companies that develop it release it in the safest state possible.

“We need something like the American FDA for drugs where the companies have to give very good statistical evidence to an independent authority that [the AI model] does more good than harm before it is released,” he declared. “We discovered with thalidomide [a drug developed in the 1950s that caused severe birth defects] that releasing and then suing them isn’t the right way to do it. What the big tech companies are saying is treat it like we treated thalidomide.”

Sign up to the Tom's AI Guide weekly newsletter summing up all the biggest AI news you need to know. Plus, analysis from our AI editors and tips on how to use the latest AI tools!

One of Hinton’s other more recent comments about AI that proved to be alarming for many was said during a CNN segment. Simply put, he warned everyone that the threat of humanity’s destruction via AI is a real possibility if we don’t get it under control. “If we don’t figure out how to make it safe, there’s a real possibility it could destroy us,” he declared. “We’ve never made things that are smarter than ourselves. We don’t know what it’s like to deal with them.” He went on to say that it would simply be “foolish to keep developing these things until we understand how we can live with them.”

Hinton’s sentiments about AI fall in line with most of the American voters who made their voices heard against the technology. According to Data Centers on the Ballot, 37 measures aimed at restricting data center development have won over a single measure that’s attached to allowing the continued expansion of those same centers.

And while the Trump administration’s push for AI and renaming it “Super Intelligence” has made the rounds, Americans are largely pushing back against those initiatives (as evidenced by this site’s current 90.5% rate of people who oppose data centers). Even with Hinton’s negative sentiments about AI while speaking with The Guardian, he remained optimistic about humanity and the ability to intervene to keep it under control.

“My gut is that we’re still in control,” he proclaimed. “We might well be able to do something. It’ll only work if we put a big effort into it, but we might well put a big effort into it.”

Market veteran indicates bounce setups on a selection of altcoins with these thresholds ought to be cleared.

Choice Hotels paid Barcelona-based Sercotel $24.3 million in September to exit an acquisition agreement, a person familiar with the transaction told Skift. Choice disclosed the payout in its latest quarterly filing without naming the company, noting it terminated the buy-out agreement on September 16. On the earnings call, CEO Dominic Dragisich said what began as an option to buy full ownership "eventually migrated to an obligation," and that Choice had expected the business to grow through franchise and management fees. Instead, growth increasingly came from leasing hotels. Dragisich described the leases as healthy but said the added lease exposure conflicted with Choice's asset-light strategy. The two companies have partnered on distribution since 2018, and Sercotel generated €170 million in revenue in 2025. The exit fee lands alongside Choice's push to recycle capital tied up in real estate: the company recently sold two hotels for about $34 million in estimated net cash proceeds while keeping their franchise contracts.

Brandweek 2026: Can Too Much Attribution Make Marketing Worse? ft. Amanda Teder

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Attribution’s a tricky thing. It can help marketers make better decisions while simultaneously making their strategy worse.

Live from the Marketing Vanguard stage at Brandweek 2026, Jenny Rooney sits down with Amanda Teder, CMO of Generac, to unpack what happens when marketing gets too focused on what is easiest to measure.

Amanda breaks down the full-funnel operating model she has built at Generac, and why demand generation is only the opening move in a high-consideration category.

She also discusses why some purchase journeys need nurturing for months or even years.

This episode is part of a special vidcast series that was recorded live during Brandweek 2026 and presented in partnership with Tatari.

Amanda Teder is Chief Marketing Officer at Generac, an energy solutions company serving both residential and industrial customers.

Before joining Generac, Amanda spent roughly 20 years at Procter & Gamble, where she worked across brands including Oral-B and Febreze, before moving to Michelin.

Across those very different categories, one theme has stayed consistent: understand what actually drives the business, then build the systems that can turn customer insight into repeatable growth. And, at Generac, that has meant connecting demand generation, nurture, sales enablement and post-purchase support into a more complete customer journey.

[07:41] Lead Generation Isn’t Your Finish Line — Amanda argues that demand generation is not enough for a considered purchase. Generac’s model follows the customer through awareness, nurture, sales support and post-purchase experience. The aim is to build a system that can turn that interest into revenue across the entire journey.

[10:05] Great CMOs Know What Kind of Marketer They Are — Amanda describes herself as a builder. She likes entering a business, identifying the critical growth levers and putting the systems in place to make those levers work repeatedly. That self-awareness matters more to her than chasing a brand because it happens to look exciting from the outside; the opportunity is in what you can build.

[13:10] The AI Debate Is Starting With the Wrong Question — At Brandweek 2026, Amanda pushes past the familiar “AI versus humans” framing. Before deciding what AI should do and what people should keep, marketers need to ask why the work exists in the first place. Strategy, prioritization, judgment and taste have to come first, otherwise, automation simply helps you do the wrong thing faster.

[14:08] Attribution Can Make Marketing Worse — Amanda’s unpopular marketing opinion is one worth sitting with: too much focus on attribution can drag investment toward the bottom of the funnel because that is where outcomes are easiest to measure. The risk is that marketers sacrifice the brand and demand-building work that creates future opportunity. Measurement should sharpen strategy, not shrink it.

Jenny Rooney is Chief Brand and Community Officer, leading strategy for the overall ADWEEK brand as well as the ways in which we serve and support our audiences with high-value content, products, partnerships and experiences, notably through our community programs such as Marketing Vanguard.

Plus, why doing the right thing beats the short-term number.

A Hiker Slid Hundreds of Feet Down Mount Timpanogos. Newly Released Video Shows the Harrowing Rescue.

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A hiker slid hundreds of feet down Utah's Mount Timpanogos while hiking in late October (Photo: UCSO)

Newly released helicopter footage shows the dramatic moment rescuers saved a hiker who fell from the summit of Mount Timpanogos, a 11,700-foot peak in Utah’s Wasatch Range.

On October 26, a female hiker fell off the trail and slid several hundred feet from the peak. She landed on a small ledge on a cliff, according to the Utah County Sheriff’s Office (UCSO). Authorities have not released the woman’s name or age.

“We have heard that the hiker is recovering well from her injuries, and we wish her a full and speedy recovery,” the agency wrote in a statement on November 3. “Thank you to all those agencies that rushed to the rescue. This incident is a stark reminder of how fast a fun outing can turn into a life-or-death situation.”

About 50 miles from Salt Lake City, Mount Timpanogos is accessible via two strenuous trails: Timpooneke and Aspen Grove. Both trails are roughly 14 miles out-and-back and cross snowfields and steep scree fields. Day hikes often take between eight and 12 hours, according to Explore Utah Valley.

While Timpanogos is a popular late-summer day hike, snowstorms can sweep through the high-elevation summit beginning in late September, making trail conditions unpredictable and sometimes dangerous.

The video shows the mountain summit where the hiker started their trek, the steep slope they slid down, and the small ledge where they stopped. According to the UCSO, search-and-rescue crews initially spotted the woman from an airplane before sending in the Utah Department of Public Safety Helicopter Crew helicopter to hoist her to an awaiting ambulance.

“The hiker was badly injured during the fall and was taken by Lone Peak Fire Department to the Utah Valley Hospital,” the UCSO wrote in an October 26 Facebook post.

The responding agencies remind hikers to use extreme caution when recreating in high elevations where snow and ice are present. In the late fall, snow can melt and refreeze, turning trails into icy ramps. Knowing how to self-arrest with an ice axe and carrying mountaineering gear is essential.

“We are extremely grateful for the outcome of today’s rescue and our awesome partners,” UCSO said.

These former members are returning to Congress next year

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Even in an election cycle marked by surprises and upsets, Capitol Hill observers will spot some familiar faces (identical twins notwithstanding) among this year’s midterm winners.

At least seven former members won comeback bids Tuesday.

The winners included alumni who dusted themselves off after election losses just two years ago. While other winners haven’t been in office in nearly two decades, their service predating the addition of a women’s restroom in the House (more recent than you may guess).

Here’s how these former members did on election night:

Carl lost a primary to fellow Republican Rep. Barry Moore in 2024 after a new map placed them in the same district. But with Moore running for the Senate in 2026, Carl had an easier path back to his old seat, which was redrawn again this year. He defeated his Democratic opponent Tuesday by 18 points.

It has been nearly two decades since Solis last served in Congress. She left in 2009 to serve as President Barack Obama’s first Labor secretary. Solis was in her sixth term as a Los Angeles county supervisor when she announced a bid for Congress after California voters approved a map that created five Democratic pickup opportunities.

Solis faced limited resistance on her path to victory in the 38th District, especially after Democratic Rep. Linda T. Sánchez opted to run for the redrawn 41st District.

Bean is making her way back to the House after losing her suburban Chicagoland seat in the conservative tea party wave of 2010.

A decade-and-a-half later, she won an eight-way primary for the 8th District seat Democrat Raja Krishnamoorthi vacated for an unsuccessful Senate bid. Winning the nomination all but guaranteed Bean a November victory in the deep-blue seat.

One of the cycle’s biggest comeback stories was in the Buckeye State, where Sherrod Brown was mounting a comeback bid two years after Republican Bernie Moreno had unseated him. Positioning himself as an opponent of data centers, Brown defeated appointed GOP Sen. Jon Husted in a special election for the remainder of Vice President JD Vance’s term. He could take office as early as December but would have to run again in 2028 if he wants another full term.

First elected in 2018, Allred left Congress two years ago after an unsuccessful Senate campaign against Republican incumbent Ted Cruz. After briefly launching a second Senate bid, Allred switched to a redrawn open House seat and won his primary over Rep. Julie Elizabeth Johnson, his successor in the chamber. Allred had no trouble winning his general election Tuesday for a fourth House term.

McAdams served one term in Congress representing a battleground Utah seat, before being unseated by Republican Burgess Owens in 2020. But after a state judge approved a new congressional map that created a heavily Democratic seat anchored in Salt Lake City, McAdams jumped into the race. He comfortably won his primary over more progressive opponents and stomped his way to a second term on Tuesday.

Once a high-ranking member of the House Armed Services Committee, Luria lost her Hampton Roads seat in 2022 to Republican Jen Kiggans.

But a week after Virginia Democrats posted strong showings in the commonwealth’s 2025 off-year elections, Luria launched her House comeback bid and went on to oust Kiggans on Tuesday.

Former House Intelligence Chairman Mike Rogers left the House after the 2014 elections and made his first Senate bid 10 years later. He lost to Democrat Elissa Slotkin but sought the seat of retiring Democratic Sen. Gary Peters the following year.

Rogers tried to take advantage of Democratic divisions between primary winner Abdul El-Sayed and Rep. Haley Stevens, even winning a late endorsement from the congresswoman. But it was not enough to stop El-Sayed on Tuesday.

Republican John E. Sununu served three terms in the House and one in the Senate before losing to Democrat Jeanne Shaheen in 2008. With Shaheen retiring, Sununu attempted a comeback but ended up losing decisively to Democratic Rep. Chris Pappas.

Two years after losing her at-large House seat to Republican Nick Begich, Peltola chose to challenge Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan. The race was rocked by late revelations by The New York Times that top aides to Peltola’s campaign were closely involved in recruiting a second candidate named Dan Sullivan into the race to peel votes from the incumbent. As of Wednesday afternoon, Peltola was clinging to a narrow lead as votes were counted.

Perriello won an upset victory in a Charlottesville-anchored district in 2008 before losing reelection in the tea party wave two years later. He challenged freshman Republican John McGuire for the current version of the seat but was narrowly trailing as of Wednesday afternoon and McGuire has already declared victory.

"Arkane is profitable," say Marvel's Blade developers in fiery new statement, "just not profitable enough for Microsoft"

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Unionised workers reiterate call for a "solid offer" for Arkane Lyon's future

Unionised workers at Dishonored developers Arkane Lyon have published a fresh statement about the progress of negotiations with Microsoft over the studio's potential closure. Aside from calling on Xbox management to make a redundancy offer proportionate to Microsoft's colossal market value, they accuse the parent company of creating the very economic conditions cited as a reason for closing Arkane, and thereby "sabotaging" development of the forthcoming Marvel's Blade.

Shared by French union Le Syndicat des Travailleureuses du Jeu Vidéo, the (slightly shonky) official English translation of the statement begins by insisting that "work on Blade is going fine" and that the game will measure up to previous Arkane Lyon releases, despite the stress of trying to pin down a new owner for the company, after Microsoft announced plans to part ways with Arkane and several other studios back in July. It then takes Microsoft to task for making an "outrageously low" redundancy offer to Arkane's workers, and further accuses the Xbox company of essentially setting Arkane up to fail by imposing a hiring freeze and depriving the studio of resources.

"Microsoft chose to justify the closure on economic reasons," the workers write. "But Microsoft is actually why those economic reasons even exist." It asserts that Microsoft froze hiring "for 19 months, which artificially caused delay and additional cost", only to then use those delays and extra costs "as a reason to close down the studio". It also claims that Microsoft have "systematically" deprived Arkane of cash, by means of corporate mechanisms the statement doesn't make wholly clear. "Even this closure itself is done against any financial logic, as closing Arkane today would probably be more expensive than finishing the game," the statement adds, without going into detail.

"The truth is that Microsoft based its decision only on stone cold and inhuman logic, in which our lives, projects and dreams are voided, so we can better serve Microsoft’s short term financial goals," the workers continue. They accuse Microsoft of "the brutal application of a never-ending search for maximum profit optimization", citing years of mass layoffs across the Xbox company together with the recent voluntary buyout offering. "Arkane is profitable," they add. "Just not profitable enough for Microsoft."

The workers want a "solid offer" from Microsoft regarding the studio's post-Xbox future, declaring that "when the company who decided that Arkane has to shut down is, according to many sources the most profitable in the world, with a net profit of more than 133 Billions of dollars in the past years, it would be the bare minimum to ensure that the redundancy plan offered by Microsoft is on [par] with their means."

If Arkane have to close, the unionised workers call on Microsoft "to release funds commensurate with Microsoft’s status as a corporation, to make sure that the workers are compensated in line with the loss suffered". They'd like a redundancy payment of €200,000, two years of "redeployment support", a package of "shared resources for training and studio creation", including a residual bonus, and "compensation up to the redundancy payment for independent workers’ contract breach", to safeguard the livelihoods of freelancers and contractors.

The warring parties at Xbox and Arkane have until 13th December this year to figure out a deal. Reportedly, Ubisoft are among those looking to buy Arkane - a source of some concern, given that Ubisoft have also recently closed a number of studios and laid many people off. I'm not sure I'd be waving my wallet around if I were using my other hand to sign pink slips.

10 Best Chain Italian Subs, According To Customer Reviews

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The Italian sub is its own category of cuisine. Born of the ingenuity of Italian immigrants, this centuries-old sandwich balances the highest forms of culinary technique with the simplest combination of ingredients. Expertly cured meats, creamy provolone cheese, crisp vegetables, and the right formula of oil and vinegar — all layered on a crusty roll, hoagie, or baguette — meld into a perfectly harmonious bite that's simultaneously salty, tangy, punchy, and fresh. Within this basic framework is room for limitless personalization. Some shops adorn their Italian sandwich with mayonnaise or hot relish. Another might add prosciutto or smoked ham or a stack of vinegary banana peppers. One may even toast the thing.

That's why the Italian sub is such a fun food to explore. No two are identical — even if they use exactly the same ingredients. Obviously, a sandwich with so much history is an easy target for dissection and judgment. Customers have their favorites, especially when it comes to what's offered at popular chain restaurants. And they're not afraid to share their feelings. For this article, I looked through hundreds of reviews, comments, and posts to find the Italian sub sandwiches with the highest customer ratings and feedback. The chains on this list range from dedicated sandwich spots to supermarkets to gas–station chains, but they all put together a great Italian sub.

With higher-quality ingredients, sliced-to-order meats, and lettuce shredded so thin it defies Newtonian mechanics, you could argue Jersey Mike's has successfully redefined the fast-casual sub. But a sandwich shop — especially one with the word "Jersey" in its name — is really only as good as its Italian sub. And in this department, Mike's Original Italian (AKA "The #13") delivers. It's a classic "Jersey-style" Italian sandwich, filled with provolone, ham, prosciuttini, capicola, salami, and pepperoni, creating a symphony of sweet, spicy, and savory meats.

"I went to Jersey Mike's for the first time and had one of the best subs I've had in a long time – #13 Italian sub done Mike's way," starts one Reddit thread. "13, Mike's way, add relish is the best Italian sandwich I have had since buying them from Italian bakeries as a kid." noted another commenter. The overall consensus is that Jersey Mike's takes pride in its product, and that getting the sandwich "Mike's Way," with a topping of tomato, onion, olive oil, red wine vinegar, oregano, and salt, elevates the experience to what you may find at a local Italian deli. It seems the community has spoken: Jersey Mike's is the best.

With over 2,500 locations across the country, Jimmy John's is one of the largest sandwich chains in the U.S., and while not all its sandwiches receive universal praise, one exception stands out: the JJ's Italian Night Club. And just like its major competitor, Jersey Mike's, Jimmy John's doesn't reinvent the wheel with its Italian sandwich. It's a classic combination of meat (salami, capicola, ham), provolone cheese, onion, lettuce, tomato, mayo, oil, vinegar, and seasonings, all served on freshly baked French bread.

Customers love the bread at Jimmy John's — just not its appearance. "Their bread looks plain AF, but it really do hit," said one Redditor. "That bread looks sad, but the Italian night club with hot peppers is the only sandwich that gets my money there," mentioned another. To deal with this boring bread, regulars recommend toasting it to achieve a light-brown crust and accentuate its yeasty aroma. The aforementioned hot peppers are another game changer, adding a tart, vinegary bite and lingering heat that cuts through the otherwise rich and meaty sandwich. One insider suggests that, when ordering the hot peppers, ask for extra.

While Jersey Mike's and Jimmy John's have thousands of locations scattered throughout the country, there are a handful of smaller regional chains serving quality, thoughtfully assembled sub sandwiches. Penn Station is one of them. With over 300 restaurants across the South and Midwest, Penn Station is winning over the hearts (and stomachs) of its customers with a concentrated menu of subs, wraps, hand-crafted sandwiches, and seasonal specials. As with most sub chains, its Classic Grilled Italian is getting the most attention. Along with the typical ingredient lineup, Penn Station uses smoked ham and banana peppers, minor tweaks that add a little electricity to its signature sandwich.

On Reddit, the sandwich receives gushing adoration from regular customers. "Penn Station's Italian sub has a hold on me," starts one thread. A lot of the love has to do with the mayonnaise, which one person described as "otherworldly." But what makes up the bulk of every sub sandwich is its bread, and here, again, Penn Station doesn't falter, with several customers noting its flavor and texture as a main contributor to the chain's overall success.

DiBella's began as a neighborhood grocery store in upstate New York around a hundred years ago. A lot has changed in the centuries since, just not the quality of its sandwiches, which have remained undeniably delicious for decades. Now, I know that we've talked a lot about bread already, but DiBella's might produce the best loaf of the bunch. The dough is aged for a minimum of 16 hours, scored by hand, and baked fresh two times a day to ensure customers never bite into something even remotely stale, which is why one enthusiastic customer called it the GOAT.

Online, people are impressed that the chain can execute mom-and-pop level taste and freshness (and an impeccable meatball sub). "DiBella's is amazing...It's hard to believe it's a chain," noted a fan. But how's the sandwich itself? Well, with a name like DiBella's, you know that its signature sandwich, aptly named "The Godfather" delivers everything you could want in a good Italian sub. All in all, it's a very solid sandwich that needs to be on your radar.

Cheba Hut's branding reads like something from a college dorm room, but its food quality is anything but juvenile or half-baked. Across the board, Cheba Hut does solid sandwiches, but its La Canna has emerged as a fan favorite, with one thread titled "My New Favorite Sub," getting over 100 upvotes on Reddit.

The La Canna distinguishes itself from other Italian subs by using prosciutto in place of the more common capicola, along with Genoa salami and smoked ham. Equally important is the ingredient balance, which pairs crisp vegetables, meat, and cheese with a tangy "shake & house" dressing. "Can't beat a La Canna on garlic herb," said one Redditor. But what makes Cheba Hut so special is its general culture of sandwich lawlessness. There are few rules here; you can get your sandwiches with alfalfa sprouts or cream cheese, on garlic bread, or served alongside a bowl of French onion soup for dipping.

Potbelly doesn't get the respect it deserves. While most of the chains on this list specialize in sandwiches, Potbelly does it all: wraps, salads, soups, subs, chili, milkshakes, mac and cheese, and one of the best club sandwiches around. But with so much variety, it's easy to be fooled into thinking it's a "jack of all trades, master of none" scenario, but this isn't the case at all.

But the Italian sandwich, although slightly more conservative than its other sandwich offering, is still tasty and reliable. Unlike other chains, Potbelly offers four — not three — heavy-hitting meats, including salami, old-world capicola, pepperoni, and mortadella — a detail that only the most observant sandwich snobs appreciate. "Their Italian is the best chain sub shop Italian out there. Nowhere else has mortadella," noted a Redditor. Others appreciate the sandwich, as well, with one person saying it's their "top choice for a commercial chain place." But what really sets this sandwich apart from the others is the giardiniera, a quintessential Chicago topping of hot pickled peppers, carrots, celery, and other veggies. The condiment is so desirable that Potbelly even sells it by the jar.

Unlike some of the other sandwich chains on this list, PrimoHoagies specializes in Italian subs. And that distinction, especially for a Philadelphia-based operation, leaves little room for error. PrimoHoagies is so committed to the Italian sandwich that it offers not one, not two, but five different variations, each with a different formula of sweet, spicy, and sharp meats and cheeses. Online, the sandwiches at PrimoHoagie's are both widely celebrated and the source of debate, specifically for the quantity of meat packed between its bread.

But many customers think this is a good problem and actually appreciate the quantity and value. In many ways, this is the most authentic sandwich of the bunch. It doesn't come with bells and whistles, just a short list of quality ingredients layered on an "award-winning seeded bread." And if you really have an issue with the quantity of cold cuts, just ask the sandwich makers for some extra veggies.

No one thinks twice about grabbing groceries at a supermarket, but for whatever reason, when it comes to prepared foods — especially sandwiches — shoppers are a little more hesitant. But when you really think about it, the supermarket, with its endless inventory of fresh vegetables, breads, meats, and cheeses, is the ideal place to grab a sub. Publix, a chain of grocery stores popular in Florida and the Southern United States, has made a name for itself in the sandwich department. Whereas other supermarkets haphazardly throw their sandwiches together, Publix constructs its subs with care and attention.

Online, customers voice their appreciation. "There are few things better in life than a Pub Sub," mentioned a Redditor. And despite being a regional supermarket chain, Publix has legions of followers across the country who will make a special trip whenever they're nearby. The supermarket's Italian sub, which comes with the classic trio of meats and provolone, isn't revolutionary, but what sets the sandwich apart is the quality of the vegetables and freshly-baked Italian Five Grain sub roll. Add some oil and vinegar for a perfectly balanced bite.

Wawa has a cult following, and for good reason. Its to-go meals, subs, sides, and secret off-menu items are terrific, even compared to fast-casual chains dedicated specifically to the craft of sandwich making. If you're unfamiliar with Wawa, you can order via a handy touchpad, where you can order sandwiches as-is or customize them to your preferences. If you suffer from choice paralysis, its predesigned subs are a terrific option. One that constantly scores high marks is its Italian sub.

In a ranking of all the Wawa subs (or hoagies if you're from Philadelphia), The Takeout declared the Italian the best option, citing its flavorful collection of meats and deft blend of salty, spicy, and fresh ingredients. This is far from a fluke. One Reddit thread dedicated to Wawa's Italian Sub has over 400 upvotes, with users describing their specific orders in the comments. Some people go standard, but many regulars like to add extra meat and cheese, jalapeños, oil and vinegar, or garlic aioli. But it's the entire Wawa experience that has won over the hearts of its customers. You can fill up your gas tank, grab a quick sandwich, and be back on the road in just a few minutes.

Another gas station moonlighting as a fast-casual stop for quality grub, QuickChek isn't so different from Wawa. And the same goes for the quality of its subs. Both chains offer a similar digital ordering platform where customers can choose every aspect of their sandwich, from the toppings to the bread. Sub sandwiches shouldn't leave you hungry, and QuickChek is a bang for your buck. But don't take our word for it. In 2021, QuickChek was named the "Best Value" for Italian subs in New Jersey, according to New Jersey Monthly.

And when you see some of its sandwiches, you'll know why. One of its Signature Subs, the Giant Italian, lives up to its name: a mammoth foot-long sub loaded with ham, salami, pepperoni, fresh mozzarella, tomato, onion, lettuce, garlic aioli, and a dynamite cherry pepper relish. On Facebook, regulars agree that for the price, speed, and accessibility, it's hard to beat a QuickChek sandwich. It's all love for this affordable convenience chain.

I used Reddit, Facebook, roundups, and other social media channels to identify the best Italian subs from popular chains. Some were obvious, but others surprised me. I kept it to chains with more than 50 locations.

If a supermarket or gas station/QSR hybrid reportedly puts together consistently good subs, it made the list. You'll notice a couple of the big names were excluded, and that's due to the fact that it seems that the quality has slipped so low that the general consensus amongst consumers, especially ones knowledgeable/passionate enough to discuss sandwiches on message boards, was unfavorable.

Qatar Launches Two South American Destinations

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Qatar Airways added two South American destinations on Wednesday, becoming the first Gulf carrier to serve Venezuela.

The new service flies from Doha to Bogotá, Colombia, then continues to Caracas, Venezuela, before returning nonstop to Doha. Qatar Airways will operate the route on Wednesdays and Sundays using a Boeing 777-200LR.

Flights are scheduled to leave Doha at 7:35 a.m. and reach Bogotá at 4:25 p.m. After a 90-minute stop, the aircraft continues to Caracas, arriving at 9 p.m. The return flight leaves Caracas at 11:30 p.m. and reaches Doha at 8:35 p.m. the following day. All times are local.

The airline originally planned to start flying the route in July but postponed the launch following an earthquake in Venezuela, according to Aviation Week.

Qatar Airways is also expanding its codeshare with LATAM to include domestic flights from Bogotá. The agreement will put Qatar’s code on LATAM flights to destinations including Medellín, Cartagena and Cali.

The additions bring Qatar Airways’ network in the Americas to 17 destinations. The carrier began serving South America in 2010 with flights to São Paulo.

Bitcoin and Ethereum ETFs shed $1.8B – Why investors are pulling back

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

The Spot Bitcoin [BTC] ETFs witnessed a sustained wave of outflows between the 30th of October and the 3rd of November, recording approximately $1.16 billion in cumulative net outflows in just three trading sessions.

According to Farside Investors data, on the 30th of October, the ETFs recorded their largest single-day outflow of the period, worth $643.2 million.

In fact, the negative momentum continued on the 2nd of November, when BTC ETFs recorded another $398.6 million in net outflows. On the 3rd of November, however, the outflow momentum eased considerably, with total withdrawals declining to $115.6 million.

In these three days, BlackRock’s IBIT alone accounted for $734 million in outflows, making it the biggest contributor to the negative trend.

On the other hand, spot Ethereum [ETH] ETFs continued to experience persistent capital outflows for seven consecutive days from the 26th of October to the 3rd of November 2026. Overall, Ethereum spot ETFs recorded approximately $656.4 million in cumulative net outflows.

Remarking on the same, Bloomberg analyst James Seyffart said,

Things were looking pretty good into the end of September but a chunk of that reversed course in October.

He further added that the average cost basis of ETF holdings stood at $3,182, compared to ETH’s market price of approximately $2,341, which left investors with aggregate unrealized losses of nearly $3.47 billion.

Meanwhile, on the altcoin front, XRP ETFs witnessed a $529.51K net outflow on the 2nd of November.

Whereas, Binance [BNB] ETFs recorded net outflows of $271.01K on 3rd November. Hyperliquid [HYPE] spot ETFs, however, stood out with positive momentum, recording $605.08K in net inflows on the same day, following substantial outflows of $2.86 million a day earlier.

In contrast, Solana [SOL] spot ETFs recorded zero flows on both days.

All this happened amidst a tough macro backdrop, where the U.S. Federal Reserve’s October decision to maintain interest rates at 3.75%–4.00% ignited concerns about persistent inflation.

Meanwhile, the 3rd November U.S. midterm elections added to market caution, while a modest recovery in the U.S. dollar further pressured dollar-denominated assets.

Topping all of this was the Binance security breach that triggered fresh anxiety across the cryptocurrency market, potentially accelerating institutional withdrawals.

We've reviewed a lot of office chairs over the years, and there's a brand we often come back to at the budget end of the spectrum. Sihoo is known for its fantastic-value M18, the cheapest ergonomic office chair we recommend, but it also makes some higher-end offerings. The Doro C300 Pro is the one that's most impressed us, and it's now reduced by $70 from $509 to $439 on the Sihoo website.

That's a lot cheaper than most of the best office chairs for back pain, and the Doro C300 Pro has a wide seat, breathable mesh, lumbar support, lots of adjustability and fantastic armrests.

The Doro C300 Pro sits firmly at the top of Sihoo's range of ergonomic office chairs and it's as good as many that cost over $100 more, as our Doro C300 Pro review notes.

The mesh material is great for breathability and has a good balance between firmness and spring. The lumbar support does its job, and the level of adjustability is impressive, particularly in the armrests, which move up/down, forward/back, tilt and can even more laterally closer or further from your chest.

There's also a footrest version, reduced from $559.99 to $479.99.

We recognise that the C300 Pro is not the most beautiful office chair, and, like all Sihoo chairs, it takes some patience with assembly. But once you've got it set up, it is very comfortable.

The highlight is the lumbar support, which is one of the more comfy that we've tested, helping to support the back's natural 's' shape without making you feel like it wants to break you into submission.

Most of the chair's adjustability is as you'd expect from a higher-mid range ergonomic chair, but the armrests stand out. They go up/down, forward/back, and tilt and, most impressively, move laterally in and out from your chest. This is no minor detail: it provides more options for how you sit and allows you to freely move your arms as you want.

Brighton and Liverpool made unfashionable tactical pivots at the weekend that could be the start of a new trend

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

If these two clubs are doing it, it's worth investigating...

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Crossing isn’t exactly a dying art, but it has certainly become rarer to see the more ambitious, attack-minded teams swing crosses into the box from open play.

It just isn’t de rigueur to try something so low-percentage, something so unfashionably anti-aesthetic.

But two of the most attacking clubs (with two of the most modern, zeitgeisty tacticians) pivoted to crossing at the weekend, so it is worth considering whether this is the beginning of a new trend that will catch on.

Fashion, after all, plays an outsized role in tactical theory in the Premier League, and if Brighton and Liverpool are doing it, then it must be Smart and Good.

Liverpool’s 12 open-play crosses in the first half on Sunday were the most any team have managed against Arsenal since November 2020. By the 42nd minute, when Alex Mac Allister should have scored with a header from what was their 19th cross (open or set-piece) of the match, Liverpool had almost quadrupled the five crosses they made in the whole game against Brighton the weekend before.

Brighton weren’t as consistent in their 2-0 victory over Manchester City, but there was a clear tactical plan to switch the play with long diagonals into the (very wide) wingers as often as possible. Fabian Hurzeler appeared to see City’s not-actually-full-backs as the weak spot, and it worked, leading directly to the winning goal.

Liverpool and Brighton had different reasons for their strategies, although the end result was the same: both knew that by getting balls into the box they could sew chaos. Brighton’s winner came via a deflected half-clearance, while Arsenal’s defence regularly made first contact but, now on the back foot, were penned in by the loose second ball (at their most dishevelled, Jurrien Timber pulled on Alexander Isak’s shirt).

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

As Iraola put it: “We’ve put them under pressure, we’ve put them inside the box, we’ve put them there.”

Arsenal, uncomfortable, were pushed back into the box; were 'put there'. It is a simple, albeit unfashionable, idea that others may choose to follow.

‘Enough Phone’ is an Android phone roughly the size of iPhone 13 Mini, launching in 2027

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Everyone says they want small Android phones back, and 2027 might be your chance to actually buy one. The “Enough Phone” has a mere 5.3-inch display, decent specs, and a price of $599 and less.

Launching in May 2027, the “Enough Phone” is a 5.3-inch Android phone that simply does want to be “enough.”

The company says the 5.3-inch LCD display comes in at 1520×720 and that the phone will be powered by a MediaTek Dimensity 7500 chipset, 8GB of RAM, and offers 128GB of storage. It’ll come with either “stock Android” or OdysseyOS, an Android skin used by SLEKE and designed to be minimal and act like a modern dumbphone. SLEKE currently sells repurposed Pixel phones running this software.

Android 17 will run out of the box with updates through 2032.

“Enough Phone” measures in at 132.8 x 63.8 x 12mm and will have a battery between 3,500 and 4,000 mAh which will be user-replaceable. While it’s thicker, the physical size is roughly the same as iPhone 13 Mini.

Rounding out the package would be a fingerprint sensor in the power button, a single 50MP (Sony IMX766) camera on the back, and a 32MP selfie camera up front.

The company says that these specs are “mostly final” and that pricing is planned for $599.

“Enough Phone” is funding on Kickstarter with $399, $449, and $499 early bird reservation tiers ahead of the May 2027 shipping estimate.
